[2026-09-01] TASK-018 - DONE
Processor: consumers for ReadingsIngested/IngestAttemptRecorded with idempotency through a
processed_messages ledger, and one-transaction ingest_batches + readings writes (PRD F3).

Design (src/Processor/WeakAppHandler.Processor.{Domain,Application,Infrastructure}/...):
  - IngestionRecorder.RecordAttemptAsync/RecordReadingsAsync both funnel through one RecordAsync:
    check processed_messages for the message id, and if absent open a transaction, insert the
    ledger row, run the message-specific persist delegate, then commit. A DbUpdateException whose
    inner exception is a Postgres unique-violation is caught and the transaction rolled back before
    anything else touches the connection (Postgres refuses further commands on an aborted
    transaction); the ledger is re-checked afterward - present means a genuine concurrent redelivery
    (report Duplicate), absent means the violation was on ingest_batches' own primary key (the two
    consumers of one batch racing to insert its row) and is rethrown so MassTransit's retry policy
    re-runs the consumer, which now finds the row and updates it instead of inserting.
  - ChangeTracker.Clear() in a finally when the transaction did not commit, because MassTransit can
    retry a faulted message on the same DbContext scope - without clearing, the retry's Add of the
    same ledger row would throw on the tracker before it ever reached the database.
  - The two messages of one poll attempt share one ingest_batches row and can arrive in either
    order: whichever lands first inserts a provisional row (ApplyReadingsAsync's has no HTTP status
    or final outcome to give it; ApplyAttemptAsync's is already authoritative), and the attempt
    record - the only one that knows the real outcome - overwrites outcome/http_status/duration_ms/
    error_message whichever way the messages arrived. IngestBatch's properties changed from init to
    set to allow this overwrite.
  - IReadingBatchWriter is the seam TASK-019/020 fill in with real normalisation; this task's
    NoOpReadingBatchWriter only exists so the message path has something to call.
  - Program.cs: replaced the placeholder Worker.cs with AddServiceMassTransit wiring both consumers
    onto the shipped ReadingsTopology queues/routing keys (same AddReadingsReceiveEndpoint helper
    TASK-012 built), and appsettings.json gained the RabbitMq config block ServiceMassTransitExtensions
    reads.

Testing - two layers, because a defect here can live in either the recorder or its wiring to the bus:
  - IngestionRecorderTests (6, calling IngestionRecorder directly against the real Postgres
    container): a redelivered ReadingsIngested writes one batch and never reaches the writer a
    second time; a redelivered IngestAttemptRecorded writes one row; a [Theory] over all five failed
    outcomes proves reading_count=0 and no readings; a writer that saves one reading and then throws
    halfway through leaves NEITHER the batch NOR the ledger row behind (proving the rollback, not
    just that nothing was attempted) and a subsequent clean retry of the same message succeeds; and
    readings-then-attempt ordering ends with one row whose duration_ms is the attempt's, not the
    readings message's provisional value.
  - IngestionConsumerTests (2, over a REAL RabbitMQ broker via the shared IntegrationTestFixture, per-
    test vhost): boots the Processor the way Program.cs does - same AddProcessorInfrastructure, same
    consumers on the same receive endpoints - with only the reading writer substituted, and publishes
    through the bus rather than calling the recorder, so a consumer left unregistered would fail
    these and pass the recorder-level tests just as happily.

One real defect found only by driving this through a bus rather than the recorder directly:
MassTransit's in-memory ITestHarness's InactivityTask is not reusable as a per-publish barrier - it
resolves once when the harness first goes idle, and awaiting it again after a second publish returns
immediately without waiting for that second delivery, so a duplicate-delivery test using it races
ahead of the very redelivery it means to observe (the assertion saw 1 consumed message where 2 were
expected, despite the recorder-level idempotency logic being entirely correct). Replaced with the
real broker plus ConsumeCounter, an IConsumeObserver that counts PostConsume by the message's own
domain MessageId and a WaitForConsumeCountAsync that polls until a specific count is reached with a
timeout - a deterministic wait tied to what actually happened rather than to the bus's overall
idleness. ProcessorHost (the real-Program.cs-wiring host) and ConsumeCounter were left in place from
the killed prior iteration that had already reached for this exact fix; only the test host and test
class built against the in-memory harness were replaced.

StyleCop). `dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln` -> 69/69 passed across all test assemblies (Docker
Desktop running; Testcontainers PostgreSQL + RabbitMQ).
  - tasks.json: TASK-018 status -> "done".

[2026-09-01] TASK-019 - DONE
Processor: meter auto-registration + payload normalisation into flat readings rows (PRD §6 F3).
Picked this over the other newly-unblocked tasks (TASK-017 admin REST, TASK-021 processing stats,
TASK-027 Notification schema) because it's the only remaining critical-priority task with satisfied
dependencies, and it fills the IReadingBatchWriter seam TASK-018 built specifically for it.

Design (src/Processor/WeakAppHandler.Processor.{Application,Infrastructure}/Ingestion/):
  - `PayloadNormalizer` (Application, pure/IO-free) - a flat `Dictionary<string,string>` mapping
    WeakApp's wire field names to `readings.metric_code` (energy/co2/pm25/humidity all identity,
    motionDetected -> motion_detected), and a `Normalize(payloadJson)` that parses the payload with
    `JsonDocument` and returns one `NormalizedMetricValue(MetricCode, ValueNumeric, ValueBool)` per
    recognised field. One flat table rather than one per meter type is safe because none of the
    observed field names collide across types (docs/weakapp-observed-response.json). Numeric vs.
    boolean is read straight off the JSON value's own `JsonValueKind` (True/False vs Number) rather
    than cross-checked against `metrics.value_kind`, keeping this normaliser self-contained - no DB
    round trip needed to classify a value. An unmapped field is skipped, not fatal: one stray field
    in an otherwise well-formed payload must not lose the rest of a meter's real readings.
  - `MeterReadingBatchWriter` (Infrastructure) - the real `IReadingBatchWriter`, replacing TASK-018's
    `NoOpReadingBatchWriter` (deleted). Per envelope: resolve-or-create the `Meter` by the natural key
    (location, meterType) - an in-call `Dictionary` cache avoids re-querying a meter seen earlier in
    the same batch (which would miss it, since it isn't saved yet, and insert a duplicate against the
    unique index) - then run `PayloadNormalizer.Normalize` and add one `Reading` per value, all
    sharing the batch's `observedAt` and `batchId`. A brand-new meter gets `FirstSeenAt =
    LastSeenAt = observedAt`; an existing one only has `LastSeenAt` advanced - this write-through-if-
    resolved-fresh, no-op-if-cached-in-this-call design is what makes "last_seen_at updates on every
    successful poll containing this meter" (criterion 2) hold without a second query.
  - `readings.is_changed` is written as `true` unconditionally for every row here - deliberately not
    this task's job. Comparing a new value against `meter_current_state` and publishing
    `ReadingStored` is TASK-020's own subject; TASK-018's own `TestReadingBatchWriter` test stand-in
    already made the identical placeholder choice for the same reason, so this isn't a new corner
    cut, just the same one restated in the real writer.
  - `IReadingBatchWriter`'s remarks doc and `ProcessorInfrastructureServiceCollectionExtensions`'s
    `TryAddScoped` registration were updated to point at `MeterReadingBatchWriter` instead of the
    deleted no-op.

Testing - two layers, split the same way TASK-018's did (pure logic vs. real database):
  - `PayloadNormalizerTests` (5, pure, no DB): air_quality's three fields map with correct numeric
    values; a `[Theory]` over both motionDetected booleans confirms the motion_detected rename;
    energy's single numeric field; an unmapped field alongside a known one is silently dropped
    without losing the known one; an empty payload object returns no values.
  - `MeterReadingBatchWriterTests` (3, real PostgreSQL via the shared `IntegrationTestFixture`,
    exactly TASK-019's three acceptance criteria): an unknown (location, meterType) auto-registers a
    `meters` row with `FirstSeenAt == LastSeenAt == observedAt`; a second poll for the same meter -
    using a **second, freshly-constructed** `MeterReadingBatchWriter` instance, matching production
    where `IReadingBatchWriter` is resolved scoped once per message delivery rather than reused
    across polls - advances `LastSeenAt` without a second `meters` row; an air_quality payload
    produces exactly 3 `readings` rows sharing one `observedAt` with the right metric codes and
    values. Each test inserts its own `ingest_batches` row first (`CreateBatchAsync` helper) because
    `readings.batch_id` is a real foreign key and, unlike `IngestionRecorderTests`, these tests call
    the writer directly rather than going through `IngestionRecorder` (which normally inserts that
    row first).

TWO real defects the tests caught, not just scaffolding friction:
  1. CA1859 on `PayloadNormalizer`'s static field: had to be declared as the concrete
     `Dictionary<string,string>` rather than `IReadOnlyDictionary<string,string>` - the analyzer
     flags the interface-typed field itself as a performance issue (devirtualized dictionary access),
     not just interface-typed *parameters* as in earlier tasks' notes.
  2. `DateTimeOffset.UtcNow` round-tripped through Postgres and back was NOT exactly equal to the
     original value in two tests - `timestamptz` has microsecond resolution but .NET's high-resolution
     clock produces sub-tick-of-a-microsecond remainders on this machine, so Npgsql silently drops a
     few hundred nanoseconds on write. `Assert.Equal(observedAt, meter.LastSeenAt)` failed by under
     500ns despite the writer's logic being correct. Fixed by truncating test-side timestamps to
     microsecond precision before use (`TruncateToMicroseconds` helper), not by loosening the writer
     or changing column precision - this is a test-fixture-vs-real-clock artifact, not a product bug,
     and worth remembering for any future test asserting exact equality on a `DateTimeOffset` that
     round-tripped through this database.

processor.slnf` -> 23/23 passed (Docker Desktop running; Testcontainers PostgreSQL). `dotnet build
WeakAppHandler.sln --no-incremental` -> 0 warnings/0 errors across all 21 projects. `dotnet test

[2026-09-01] TASK-020 - DONE
Processor: meter_current_state comparison (is_changed), current-state upsert, ReadingStored
publishing with value/previousValue/isChanged (PRD §6 F3).

Design (MeterReadingBatchWriter, extending TASK-019's writer rather than a separate class, since
change detection has to happen per value in the same loop that flattens the payload):
  - ApplyCurrentStateAsync compares each normalised value against meter_current_state keyed on
    (meter_id, metric_code): no prior row means IsChanged=true with PreviousValue=null (nothing to
    compare a metric's first observation against); a prior row compares ValueNumeric/ValueBool,
    always advances observed_at, and only moves changed_at when the value actually differs -
    matching the acceptance criterion that a repeated identical poll still advances observed_at
    while leaving readings.is_changed false.
  - Per-batch (meterId, metricCode) cache alongside TASK-019's per-batch meter cache, for the same
    reason: a current-state row created earlier in this same WriteAsync loop has not been saved yet,
    so a second query within the batch would miss it.
  - WriteAsync's return type changed from a row count to IReadOnlyList<ReadingStored> - one event per
    metric written, built from the same comparison that decided is_changed, so the event's
    value/previousValue/isChanged can never disagree with what was actually persisted.
  - IngestionRecorder.RecordAsync's persist delegate now returns the events and publishes them via
    IPublishEndpoint strictly AFTER transaction.CommitAsync succeeds - publishing before commit could
    announce a reading a later rollback then erases, and a message already handed to the broker
    cannot be un-published. An attempt-only message (ApplyAttemptAsync) returns an empty list: it
    carries no readings of its own to announce.
  - ReadingsTopology gained StoredRoutingKey/StoredQueueName ("readings.stored") and
    ReadingsTopologyExtensions now configures ReadingStored as a published message on the shared
    topic exchange - publish side only, since no permanent consumer exists yet (that is the
    Notification service's rule engine, TASK-029).

Testing:

    (meter, metric) pair is changed with no previous value; an identical second poll is unchanged but
    still advances meter_current_state.observed_at while changed_at stays put; a changed second poll
    flips is_changed, carries the old value forward as previousValue, and moves changed_at.

    assertions against an in-memory MassTransit harness backing IPublishEndpoint
    (IngestionRecorderTestBus): a redelivered message publishes nothing a second time, a failed
    attempt publishes nothing at all, and - the one that actually matters for the commit-then-publish
    guarantee - a writer that fails halfway publishes NOTHING, while the clean retry that follows
    publishes exactly once. Deliberately NOT the in-memory ITestHarness.InactivityTask pattern
    TASK-018's entry flagged as unreliable across multiple deliveries; these assertions read
    Harness.Published directly after an awaited in-process call returns, which carries no such race.
  - IngestionConsumerTests (real RabbitMQ) gained a new end-to-end test: publishes a ReadingsIngested

    readings.stored routing key via a test-only ReadingStoredCollectorConsumer bound in ProcessorHost,

    `MeterCurrentValueReadModel` (plain read models, NOT a reference to Processor.Domain - the
    Gateway must not take a compile-time dependency on the service that owns/migrates the schema)
    and `IGatewayReadContext` (the only seam onto the schema: `IQueryable<MeterReadModel> Meters`,
    `IQueryable<ReadingReadModel> Readings`, plus an async `GetCurrentValuesAsync` batch method for
    the DataLoader below - deliberately NOT an IQueryable, since it exists to be keyed by a

  - `WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Infrastructure/Persistence/`: `GatewayReadDbContext` (its own

    intentionally a SEPARATE EF model, not a shared assembly, so the two services' schemas can only
    drift by someone breaking the actual database, not by a shared C# type; defaults
    `QueryTrackingBehavior.NoTracking` in `OnConfiguring` so every query is no-tracking by
    construction, not by remembering `.AsNoTracking()` at every call site), `GatewayReadContext`

TWO REAL BUGS found by the one `dotnet test gateway.slnf` run that got to execute before the
blocker below hit, both fixed:
  1. `ReadingsQueryTests`' seed originally called `SeedMeterWithReadingsAsync` TWICE for the same
     (location, meterType) pair (once for the 15 in-window readings, once for the "3 hours older"
     one) - `meters` has a unique index on exactly that pair, so the second call's insert threw
     `23505 duplicate key`. Fixed by adding `SeedReadingsForExistingMeterAsync` (readings only, no
     new meter row) and using it for the "older" batch instead of seeding a second meter.
  2. `MetersQueryTests` failed with `{"errors":[{"message":"Unexpected Execution Error"}]}` - no
     detail, because `WebApplicationFactory` does not default to the `Development` environment the
     way `dotnet run` does, so HotChocolate's exception-detail masking (tied to
     `IsDevelopment()`) was hiding whatever the real exception was. Fixed two ways: `GatewayApi
     Factory.Create` now calls `.UseEnvironment("Development")` explicitly, and `Program.cs` now
     calls `.ModifyRequestOptions(o => o.IncludeExceptionDetails = builder.Environment.
     IsDevelopment())` so the underlying HotChocolate error, once unmasked, is actually visible in
     the GraphQL response instead of only in server-side logs.
     NOTE ON HOW THIS GOT FIXED: partway through this session the PowerShell tool wedged (see
     blocker below) before the masked error's REAL cause could be confirmed by re-running the
     tests. While investigating with the Edit/Read tools (which kept working), several files -
     `Program.cs` (gained the `ModifyRequestOptions` line above), `MeterReadModel.cs` and
     `ReadingReadModel.cs` (converted from positional records to records with `required { get;
     init; }` properties, with a remark explaining why: HotChocolate's `[UseProjection]` rewrites
     the SQL projection per requested GraphQL field by building a member-init expression
     `new T { Field = ... }`, which needs a parameterless constructor - a positional record's
     synthesised constructor is not one, so `[UseProjection]` over a positional-record `IQueryable`
     is a highly plausible root cause for exactly this "Unexpected Execution Error") - changed ON
     DISK without this agent making those edits. Whether that was another concurrent process
     working the same branch/task, or some other mechanism, is unclear and could not be
     investigated (no shell access - see blocker). The changes found were read carefully and are
     correct/consistent with this task's own design, so they were kept rather than reverted, per
     the standing instruction to trust unexplained on-disk changes unless they look wrong. One
     inconsistency THIS agent found and fixed after that: `GatewayReadContext.cs`'s `Meters`/
     `Readings` still called the OLD positional constructors (`new MeterReadModel(m.Id, ...)`),
     which no longer compiles against the new init-only record shapes - updated both to
     object-initializer syntax (`new MeterReadModel { Id = m.Id, ... }`) to match.
     WHETHER THIS FULLY EXPLAINS THE ORIGINAL FAILURE IS STILL UNCONFIRMED - nothing could be
     rebuilt or re-run after this fix (see blocker). The next iteration's FIRST action should be
     `dotnet build gateway.slnf` (confirm it still compiles after the hand-reconciliation above)
     then `dotnet test gateway.slnf` and read the now-unmasked error if `MetersQueryTests` still
     fails. If the real error turns out to be unrelated to `[UseProjection]`/positional records,
     the next-best suspect is the `MeterCurrentValuesDataLoader`/`AddDataLoader<T>` wiring (the
     only other genuinely new/untested-elsewhere pattern in this change) - as a safe fallback,
     `[UseProjection]` can simply be dropped from both `Query.GetMeters`/`GetReadings` without
     reopening the "no over-fetching" acceptance criterion, since `GatewayReadContext`'s hand-written
     `Select`/`Join` already narrows the SQL to just the read model's own columns independently of
     that attribute.

[2026-09-01] TASK-023 - DONE
Gateway: HotChocolate GraphQL project with a read-only NoTracking DbContext over the core schema;
meters and readings queries with projection, filtering/sorting, and Relay pagination (PRD §6 F4).

This picks up a killed iteration's WIP: the production code (GraphQLserver wiring, read models,
GatewayReadDbContext/GatewayReadContext, entity configurations, the gateway Postgres role, solution/
slnf wiring) and the test project skeleton (factory, seeding helper, GraphQL client, both query test
classes) were already written and structurally sound; my own work was diagnosing and fixing the two
real bugs a first `dotnet build`/`dotnet test` pass surfaced (below), verifying the fix's stability,
and closing out the task (tasks.json/progress.txt/commit/PR/merge).

Design:
  - GatewayReadDbContext forces UseQueryTrackingBehavior(QueryTrackingBehavior.NoTracking) in
    OnConfiguring - the Gateway only ever SELECTs from the core schema Processor owns and migrates,
    never CREATE/ALTER/INSERT/UPDATE, so there is deliberately no Gateway.Domain layer either.
  - Query.GetMeters/GetReadings return IQueryable<ReadModel> with [UseProjection][UseFiltering]
    [UseSorting] (readings also [UsePaging(MaxPageSize = 100, DefaultPageSize = 20)]), so a GraphQL
    field selection narrows the SQL projection instead of over-fetching, and a pageSize above 100 is
    a validation error rather than a large result (the second acceptance criterion, verified end to
    end).
  - GatewayReadContext.Readings joins meters in at the IQueryable level (not a nested GraphQL field)
    so `readings(where: { location: ... })` becomes one SQL WHERE on a joined column, not a second
    round trip per row; ordered newest-first by (observedAt, Id) so the cursor stays stable without
    the caller supplying an explicit sort.
  - The `currentValues` field on a meter (meter_current_state, PRD's "meters ... with their current
    values") is a batched GreenDonut DataLoader (MeterCurrentValuesDataLoader) rather than a per-meter
    query, since [UseProjection] alone cannot cover a nested field that hits a different table.

Two real bugs found by the first `dotnet build`/`dotnet test` pass, both root-caused and fixed:
  1. HotChocolate's [UseProjection] middleware rewrites the SQL projection per GraphQL field selection
     by building a member-init expression (`new T { Field = ... }`), which needs a parameterless
     constructor - MeterReadModel/ReadingReadModel were positional records, whose synthesized
     constructor takes every parameter, so every `meters`/`readings` query failed at runtime with
     "Type ... does not have a default constructor", HotChocolate's error swallowed to
     "Unexpected Execution Error" until Program.cs was given
     `.ModifyRequestOptions(o => o.IncludeExceptionDetails = builder.Environment.IsDevelopment())`
     (kept - a reasonable standing dev aid, not just a debugging scratch change) to surface the real
     message. Fixed by converting both read models to init-only-property records (no positional
     parameters), which do get a synthesized parameterless constructor, and updating
     GatewayReadContext's two `.Select`/`Join` projections to object-initializer syntax.
  2. ReadingsQueryTests seeded its (location, meter_type) rows through `private const string`
     location names shared across the class's three [Fact]s. xUnit only serialises different test
     CLASSES against each other when they share a [Collection] fixture (which is why every other test
     in this codebase generates a fresh Guid/unique-suffixed location per test) - it makes no such
     guarantee between [Fact]s within one class, and this suite's Facts do run with genuine
     concurrency against the one real Postgres container IntegrationTestFixture hands the whole
     collection, so two Facts' InitializeAsync calls raced to insert the same natural key and both
     lost to `ix_meters_location_meter_type`. Fixed by generating the two location names once per
     test-class instance (`$"...{Guid.NewGuid():N}"` on instance fields, not const), matching the
     pattern IngestionRecorderTests/MeterReadingBatchWriterTests already established. Confirmed fixed
     by re-running the Gateway test project three times in a row with zero failures (a
     concurrency-dependent bug proves itself absent by repetition, not by reading the diff).

VERIFIED: `dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln` -> 0 warnings / 0 errors (TreatWarningsAsErrors +
StyleCop). `dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln` -> 86/86 passed across all seven test assemblies (Docker
Desktop running; Testcontainers PostgreSQL + RabbitMQ), plus three additional standalone runs of
just WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api.Tests to rule out the flakiness the second bug's nature implied.
  - tasks.json: TASK-023 status -> "done".

[2026-09-02] TASK-017 - DONE
Ingestor admin REST: GET /api/v1/ingestion/status, POST /trigger, PUT /config behind a machine JWT
with scope ingestion:admin, plus the in-memory circuit-breaker/error-counter state they report
(PRD §6 F1).

Resumed from a killed iteration that had written exactly one thing: the ServicePolicies doc/constant
for the new policy. Everything below is this session's work.

The state TASK-016's note said did not exist yet (src/Ingestor/.../Polling/):
  - IngestionRuntimeState (singleton): last attempt, failure counts BY OUTCOME, total polls, last
    success instant, and the interval currently in force. Guarded by one lock and read through a
    single Snapshot() rather than per-property getters, so a status response cannot report an
    outcome from one attempt beside counters that already include the next.
  - StateRecordingIngestionPoller: a decorator around IngestionPoller, registered as IIngestionPoller
    so BOTH the timer loop and the admin trigger record through it and neither call site can forget.
    A poll that throws is deliberately not counted - every outcome WeakApp can produce comes back as
    an IngestAttemptRecorded, so an exception means the attempt died outside the pipeline and has no
    outcome that any ingest_batches row will ever corroborate.
  - IngestionWorker now re-reads the interval from that state each cycle instead of capturing it
    once, which is what makes PUT /config effective; a change lands on the next cycle rather than
    cutting short a delay already committed to.
  - Circuit-breaker state is Polly's own: WeakAppResiliencePipelineFactory now takes a
    CircuitBreakerStateProvider (registered singleton, attached to the breaker) and /status reads
    CircuitState off the live pipeline. Deliberately not mirrored into IngestionRuntimeState via
    OnOpened/OnClosed callbacks - a copy kept in step by events is only ever a stale second source
    of truth. Note the provider can be attached to exactly one breaker, which is safe only because
    the pipeline is built once per host; enabling options reloads would need one provider per build.
  - PUT /config only moves the polling interval. The attempt/total timeouts and breaker thresholds
    are what WeakAppOptionsValidator checks against each other at startup, so letting an admin move
    one independently could put the pipeline's total budget above the interval. The interval itself
    is validated against the same invariant (> total timeout) plus a 3600s ceiling, so a value in
    milliseconds by mistake is a 400 rather than ingestion silently parked for weeks. In-memory by
    design: a restart returns to configured values instead of keeping an override nobody remembers.

A REAL DEFECT in the shared auth wiring, found because this is the first task where a service has to
validate a token the Auth Service actually issued: ServiceAuthenticationExtensions configured
JwtBearer with an Authority *or* a JwksUri, but never a ValidIssuer. Authority is unusable here (the
Auth Service publishes JWKS but no OpenID discovery document, so there is no metadata to learn the
issuer from), and with issuer validation on by default and no issuer configured, EVERY real token
would have been rejected at runtime with IDX10204 - on every service, not just this one. Nothing had
caught it because no service had yet been given a protected endpoint to call with a real token. Fixed
by honouring an Auth:Issuer setting, and the Ingestor's appsettings.json now carries the full Auth
section (Issuer/Audience/JwksUri/RequireHttpsMetadata) that services need.

Also in ServiceDefaults:
  - The IngestionAdmin policy checks scope membership rather than RequireClaim("scope", ...): OAuth2
    puts every granted scope in ONE space-separated claim, so an exact-value match would reject a
    token that carries any additional scope. RequireAuthenticatedUser() is explicit so a missing
    token is a 401 challenge and a valid-but-unscoped one is a 403.
  - JwksKeyCache now takes IHttpClientFactory instead of a "new HttpClient()" it held forever (a
    singleton-held client never picks up a DNS change - the exact failure IHttpClientFactory exists
    to prevent), creating a short-lived client per refresh over a pooled handler. This is also the
    seam that lets a test route the JWKS fetch into an in-process Auth Service.
  - DevBypass principals now carry a scope claim too (configurable, default ingestion:admin).
    Without it the dev bypass would leave scope-based policies unreachable while role-based ones are
    wide open, which is a confusing local-dev failure rather than a security boundary - the bypass
    already authenticates every request as an admin.

Testing - 7 new tests in tests/WeakAppHandler.Ingestor.Tests/IngestionAdminEndpointsTests.cs, run
against BOTH real services (IngestorAdminHost boots the Ingestor via WebApplicationFactory over its
own Program.cs, and the real Auth Service against the fixture's PostgreSQL):
  - Tokens are minted by the REAL client-credentials grant, not hand-signed with a key the Ingestor
    was told to trust - the latter proves only that a JWT validates, not that the two services agree
    on issuer, audience, claim shape and key. The Ingestor then fetches the JWKS over HTTP from the
    Auth Service through TestServer.CreateHandler, so signature validation resolves a key the Auth
    Service really published; only the transport under that fetch is in-memory, because two in-process
    test servers have no port to reach each other on.
  - Only WeakApp itself is faked, and only at the HttpMessageHandler level (RecordingHandler), so the
    REAL WeakAppClient and its REAL resilience pipeline stay in the request path. That is what makes
    the circuit-breaker test meaningful: 502s drive the actual production breaker open and /status
    then reports Open, rather than the test setting a flag the endpoint echoes back.
  - Coverage: 401 without a token; 403 on all three routes for a valid VIEWER token (authentication
    succeeds, authorization must still refuse a human's token on a machine surface); /trigger reaching
    WeakApp again and answering with that batch's outcome in well under a second; /status reporting
    the SCHEDULED poll's outcome, reading count, batch id, empty failure map, Closed breaker and the
    interval in force; failures counted by reason with the breaker Open; PUT /config taking effect as
    seen through /status; and an interval below the timeout budget rejected with 400 leaving the
    interval unchanged.
  - The Ingestor test collection now shares IntegrationTestFixture (PostgreSQL + RabbitMQ) instead of
    the RabbitMQ fixture alone. The old comment "the Ingestor has no database, so PostgreSQL would be
    setup with no test behind it" no longer holds: the Auth Service that guards this API does have one.
    ingestor.slnf gained the Auth and IntegrationTesting projects to match.
  - Ingestor Program.cs gained AddControllers/UseAuthentication/UseAuthorization/MapControllers, and
    launchSettings an applicationUrl (5095) now that the service actually serves HTTP.

VERIFIED: "dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln" -> 0 warnings / 0 errors (TreatWarningsAsErrors +
StyleCop). "dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln" -> 93/93 passed across all seven test assemblies. The
Ingestor assembly (28 tests) was run four times in total - the new suite asserts on wall-clock
latency and on a breaker opening, so repetition is the only thing that shows those are not flaky.
  - tasks.json: TASK-017 status -> "done".

date_trunc/generate_series bucketing) and TASK-025 are both high-priority and extend TASK-023's
Gateway project, so they are the natural continuation; TASK-027 is the other high-priority one.
Whoever takes TASK-024 should know the read-model lesson from TASK-023: HotChocolate's [UseProjection]
needs a parameterless constructor on the projected type, so aggregation read models must be
init-only-property records rather than positional ones.

[2026-09-02] TASK-027 - DONE
Notification alerting schema: AlertingDbContext over alert_rules / alerts / alert_rule_state, EF Core
migration, and the PRD §6.6 seed rule set (PRD §7.1 plus the per-meter cooldown the architecture
review called for).

Picked over TASK-024/025 (the other two unblocked high-priority tasks) because it is the head of the
only chain that still leads to critical work: TASK-028 (pure rule engine) and TASK-029 (ReadingStored
consumer) are both critical and both blocked on nothing but this schema, whereas TASK-024/025 extend
an already-working Gateway.

Schema, in src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api/{Domain,Persistence}:
  - alert_rules carries §7.1's columns plus a nullable meter_type, which §6.6's rule model lists but
    the §7.1 table omits; null means "any" for both location and meter_type. operator/severity/status
    are enums persisted through explicit ValueConverters to the lower-case codes the PRD documents
    (gt/gte/lt/lte/eq, info/warning/critical, active/resolved) rather than EnumToStringConverter's
    PascalCase - these columns are part of TASK-030's REST contract and of what an operator reads in
    psql, so the two spellings must not diverge.
  - alerts has an FK to alert_rules (same owner) and none at all on meter_id, and carries its own
    location/meter_type/metric_code, so the feed filters without ever joining into the Processor's
    core schema. Added triggered_value_bool/resolved_value_bool next to §7.1's numeric columns: the
    seed rule set includes a boolean rule (motion in the Garage), so a numeric-only value column
    could not record what actually triggered it. FK behaviour is Restrict, not Cascade - deleting a
    rule must not erase the history raised under it. NOTE FOR TASK-030: that makes DELETE of a rule
    that has ever fired fail at the database; disabling through is_enabled is the intended path, and
    that endpoint should either return a 409 or soft-delete rather than surfacing a 500.
  - alert_rule_state is keyed on (rule_id, meter_id, metric_code) - the composite key IS the cooldown
    scope, which is the whole point of the table (a breach in the Kitchen must not silence one in the
    Garage). It also persists was_breaching so transition detection survives a restart rather than
    depending on the previousValue that happens to be on the next event. alert_rules.last_triggered_at
    is still there because §7.1 lists it, but it is documented as display-only: TASK-029 must read
    cooldown from alert_rule_state, not from the rule.
  - Three check constraints, all defence-in-depth for TASK-028/030: exactly one of
    threshold_numeric/threshold_bool set (a rule with neither reaches the engine un-evaluatable, and
    the symptom of that is a MISSING alert - the hardest failure to notice in an alerting system),
    cooldown_seconds >= 0, hysteresis_percent in [0, 100]. Plus a check that resolved_at is set iff
    status is not active.
  - PRD §7.1 asks for a partial index on status = 'active'; it is created UNIQUE on
    (rule_id, meter_id, metric_code) WHERE status = 'active'. §6.6 allows at most one active alert per
    rule and meter, so the database, not just the consumer's logic, now refuses the second one - a
    duplicate delivery or a concurrent consumer cannot produce it.

A REAL correctness bug found while reading the first scaffolded migration, not a hypothetical one:
EF omits from its INSERT any property value equal to the sentinel (by default the CLR default) when
the column has a store default and lets the database fill it in. All three of §7.1's defaulted
columns are affected, and each failure is silent: hysteresis 0 -> stored as 5.00, cooldown 0 ->
stored as 300, and is_enabled false -> stored as TRUE, so TASK-030 creating a disabled rule would
have produced an enabled one. Fixed by pointing each property's sentinel at its own default value
(HasSentinel(5.00m) / (300) / (true)): the omission then writes the identical number, while 0/0/false
are sent explicitly. HasSentinel(null) does not work here - EF rejects it for non-nullable value
types. Two tests pin this down (zero/zero/false round-trip; untouched values still take the PRD
defaults).
  - HasData does NOT honour that sentinel - it is design-time and drops seed values equal to the CLR
    default independently - so the Garage rule's deliberate hysteresis of 0 was still being left to
    the column default. Fixed with the single hand-edit made to the scaffolded migration file
    (hysteresis_percent written explicitly in that row's InsertData, commented in place). The model
    snapshot already records 0, so the database and the snapshot agree and no phantom migration
    follows.

Seed rules use EF HasData with fixed ids and a fixed timestamp, following the AuthSeedData/
MetricSeedData precedent: idempotent by construction (applied once, in the migration that creates the
table), with no startup "does this rule exist yet" scan, and an operator who deletes a seed rule
through TASK-030 keeps it deleted. Metric codes are the Processor's normalised ones (motion_detected,
not WeakApp's wire name motionDetected - see PayloadNormalizer), and "Garage" is a location the live
WeakApp really returns (docs/weakapp-observed-response.json).

Wiring: AddNotificationPersistence (Npgsql + snake_case + ConnectionStrings:Notification, throws when
missing, registers AddDbContextCheck tagged "ready") called from Program.cs; appsettings.json gained
the connection string with the same placeholder-credential convention as Processor/Auth/Gateway; a
`notification` role was added to deploy/postgres/init/01-create-service-roles.sql (TASK-043 still owns
the real least-privilege narrowing). Program.cs gained `public partial class Program;` so
WebApplicationFactory can boot the real host, as Auth and Gateway already do.

Note on the shared migration history: this is the third DbContext against the one `weakapphandler`
database and, like Auth's and the Processor's, it uses the default __EFMigrationsHistory table. That
is safe - EF ignores history rows whose migration id it does not know, and the three table sets are
disjoint - and deviating for this one context would be more surprising than consistent. Worth
remembering only if TASK-047 ever splits the services onto separate databases.

Testing - new tests/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api.Tests (15 tests, added to WeakAppHandler.sln and
to notification.slnf along with the IntegrationTesting harness), all against a real PostgreSQL
container through the shared IntegrationTestFixture, never an in-memory provider - a check
constraint, a partial unique index and a store default are exactly the things an in-memory provider
does not have:
  - schema: the three tables exist; the FKs on alerts/alert_rule_state reference alert_rules and
    nothing else (asserted on the referenced table names from information_schema, not on a count, so
    a future FK into meters would fail it); the active-alert index is UNIQUE and filtered.
  - seed: the five §6.6 rules read back off the database (metric/operator/threshold/severity), the
    Garage rule as a boolean rule with hysteresis 0, and a second MigrateAsync leaving five - checked
    by name as well as by id, since a duplicate would carry a new id and slip past a count on ids.
  - persistence: an alert written for a meter id this database has never seen (proving nothing
    requires the Processor's row) reads its location/meter_type/metric_code straight back; a second
    ACTIVE alert for the same (rule, meter, metric) is rejected while a new one after a resolve is
    allowed; two meters under one rule keep separate alert_rule_state rows with independent
    last_triggered_at.
  - host: the real Program.cs booted twice in a row, each start answering /health/ready with 200 and
    the seed rule set still five afterwards.

[2026-09-02] TASK-028 - DONE
Pure RuleEngine component: PRD 6.6's evaluation semantics (transition triggering, hysteresis,
per-(rule,meter,metric) cooldown, explicit resolution) as a single pure function, plus 54 unit tests
including the architectural test that proves the project stays reference-free.

Picked because it was the only unblocked critical-priority task (TASK-027 landed its dependency last
session) and TASK-029 sits directly behind it.

Shape, in src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.RuleEngine (8 files, one type each per this
repo's SA1402/SA1649 convention):
  - Its own pure input types - RuleOperator / RuleDefinition / MetricObservation /
    RuleEvaluationState - because the project must not reference the Api's domain types or Contracts.
    RuleOperator duplicates AlertOperator's members deliberately; the Api will map between them
    (TASK-029), and the duplication is what keeps a persistence concern from leaking into the engine.
  - ThresholdNumeric and MetricObservation.Numeric are decimal, not double: the threshold column is
    numeric(12,4), and comparing a binary float against it would make the engine and the database
    disagree about what "exactly at the threshold" means - one of the two boundary cases the task
    explicitly asks to be tested. TASK-029 converts ReadingStored's double once, at the boundary.
  - No clock: MetricObservation.ObservedAt is also the instant cooldown is measured from, so every
    decision is reproducible from its arguments alone. That is what makes the boundary cases
    arithmetic rather than integration scenarios.
  - AlertRuleEngine.Evaluate returns RuleDecision { Kind, Reason, IsBreaching }. Kind is what to do
    (None/Raise/Resolve); Reason (Raised/Resolved/NotApplicable/NoTransition/AlertAlreadyActive/
    CooldownActive/WithinHysteresisBand/NotBreaching) exists because "no alert" has seven very
    different causes, and telling them apart is the difference between a working suppression policy
    and a lost alert - the tests assert on Reason, since the mere absence of an alert satisfies every
    branch equally. IsBreaching is returned rather than left to the caller to recompute: it is exactly
    what belongs in alert_rule_state.was_breaching, and a caller deriving it a second time could
    disagree with the decision it is storing it alongside.
  - AlertRuleEngine.Matches covers rule scoping (enabled + metric + optional location/meterType where
    null means any). Case-insensitive on purpose: those strings pass through the wire payload, the
    Processor's meter registration and an operator typing into the TASK-030 form, and a rule that
    matches nothing because of a capital letter produces no alerts AND no errors.

Four decisions worth knowing before TASK-029 builds on this:
  1. wasBreaching is read from persisted state, NOT recomputed from the event's previousValue the way
     PRD 6.6's pseudocode does. After a restart the previous value is whatever the next poll happens
     to report, while the stored flag still knows the window is open - so the pseudocode's version
     would re-raise an alert for a breach that is already active.
  2. A transition swallowed by cooldown still records the breach, so the alert is DROPPED, not queued.
     The alternative (leaving was_breaching false) fires the moment the window closes, which turns a
     rate limit into a delayed duplicate. Pinned by
     Evaluate_BreachSwallowedByCooldown_DoesNotFireLateOnTheNextReading.
  3. The hysteresis band's direction follows the operator: an upper bound (gt/gte) clears downwards at
     threshold - margin, a lower bound (lt/lte) clears upwards at threshold + margin, and eq clears
     symmetrically once |value - threshold| >= margin. Getting the sign wrong on the lt family is the
     classic form of this bug and it is SILENT - the band lands on the breaching side, so the alert
     resolves the instant it stops breaching and hysteresis appears to be configured while doing
     nothing. The margin is a percentage of the threshold's MAGNITUDE (Math.Abs), so a negative
     threshold keeps its band on the correct side; a zero threshold has a zero band by construction.
  4. NotApplicable is a third answer, distinct from breaching and not-breaching, and it leaves
     was_breaching untouched instead of writing false - clearing it there would fake a transition on
     the next reading that can actually be compared. It covers: a numeric rule against a boolean
     metric and the reverse, a rule with both or neither threshold (the check constraint should
     prevent that - the engine refuses to guess rather than hide a bypassed constraint), and any
     ordering operator on a boolean rule. That last one matters: `false < true` is true in CLR terms,
     so a naive comparison would make an `lt true` rule on motion breach on every quiet poll.

Boolean metrics have no band at all - they resolve as soon as the value stops matching, whatever
hysteresis_percent happens to say, so a stray margin cannot trap the Garage motion alert open forever.

Testing - new tests/WeakAppHandler.Notification.RuleEngine.Tests (54 tests, added to
WeakAppHandler.sln and to notification.slnf), xunit only, no infrastructure of any kind:
  - RuleEngineDriver feeds a sequence of observations through the engine keyed on (meterId,
    metricCode) - the same composite key as alert_rule_state - carrying state forward the way
    TASK-029's consumer will have to. That is what makes the per-meter cooldown scope observable in a
    unit test: a suppression leaking across meters shows up here as a missing alert rather than only
    in an integration test. TASK-029 can read the driver as the reference state machine
    (was_breaching = IsBreaching; the active flag flips on Raise/Resolve; last_triggered_at advances
    only on Raise).
  - Transitions: eight polls of which seven breach produce exactly one Raise (level-triggered
    alerting would produce seven); threshold boundaries for gt/gte/lt/lte asserted from both sides at
    +/-0.0001; the resolve path fires once and then stays quiet.
  - Hysteresis: an oscillating series produces one Raise and zero Resolves; the 950 edge of a
    threshold-1000/5% rule asserted at 949.9999 / 950 / 950.0001; the lt-family band asserted above
    its threshold; zero margin, zero threshold, negative threshold and the symmetric eq band covered.
  - Cooldown: the 300s boundary at 299/300/301, per-meter independence, never-triggered-before,
    negative cooldown treated as none, and the swallowed-not-deferred case above.
  - Architecture: two complementary checks, because neither alone is sufficient. The csproj is parsed
    (XDocument) and asserted to declare no PackageReference/ProjectReference/Reference/
    FrameworkReference - the compiler drops unused references from the assembly manifest, so a
    declared-but-idle dependency would be invisible at runtime. And the loaded assembly's
    GetReferencedAssemblies must be entirely System*/netstandard/mscorlib - references inherited from
    Directory.Build.props never appear in this csproj at all, which is how the second check also
    confirms (rather than assumes) that the repo-wide StyleCop.Analyzers reference contributes no
    runtime dependency.

Deliberately NOT done here, to keep this task's scope: no mapping from the Api's AlertRule/
AlertRuleState onto the engine's records, and no consumer. That is TASK-029, and whoever takes it
needs to map AlertOperator -> RuleOperator, ReadingStored's MetricValue (double?/bool?) ->
MetricObservation (decimal?/bool?), and alert_rule_state plus "is there an active alert" ->
RuleEvaluationState, then persist the RuleDecision exactly as RuleEngineDriver does.

Resumed from an iteration whose shell tool died mid-session (every command, down to "echo hello",
returning exit 66 with empty output - the same environment failure TASK-020's entry recorded). It had
finished the src/ side and written the tests but could never build, run or commit them. This session

rationale below is that iteration's and is preserved because none of it is obvious from the diff.

Implementation - src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api/Alerting/ (7 files):
  - AlertEvaluator: loads the enabled rules, filters them through AlertRuleEngine.Matches, loads
    alert_rule_state and the open alerts for this (meter, metric), evaluates each rule, and writes
    alerts + state in ONE SaveChanges so an alert and the state row saying it was raised cannot
    disagree. Returns AlertRaised/AlertResolved instead of announcing them.
  - ReadingStoredConsumer: IConsumer<ReadingStored>, dispatching what the evaluator returned.
    Dispatch lives here rather than in the evaluator precisely so it can only happen after the commit
    - a subscriber told about an alert a rollback then erased has no way to un-see it.
  - IAlertDispatcher + LoggingAlertDispatcher: the in-process seam the task asks for. No second
    RabbitMQ exchange, because the only subscriber is this same process's future SignalR hub;
    publishing to an exchange whose sole consumer is the publisher buys nothing. TryAddSingleton so
    TASK-031 replaces the default without touching AddAlerting.
  - AlertRuleMapping, AlertEvaluationResult, AlertingServiceCollectionExtensions.
  Program.cs calls AddAlerting() and AddServiceMassTransit with the consumer bound to
  ReadingsTopology.StoredQueueName/StoredRoutingKey; appsettings.json gained a RabbitMq section; the
  csproj gained an explicit MassTransit reference (the consumer is an adapter that lives here, same
  reasoning as Processor.Infrastructure's).

Decisions worth not undoing without reading why:
  1. Rule scoping is decided in memory by AlertRuleEngine.Matches, NOT filtered in SQL. Matching is
     case-insensitive in the engine; a SQL `metric_code = @p` would quietly disagree, and the symptom
     of that disagreement is a MISSING alert with no error anywhere. The query narrows on is_enabled
     only. The rule set is hand-authored and tiny; if it stops being tiny the answer is a cache
     invalidated by TASK-030's CRUD, not a filter that can drift from the engine.
  2. Deliberately NO processed_messages ledger in this service. A redelivered ReadingStored is
     harmless by construction: the second evaluation reads was_breaching = true and an open alert from
     the database, so the engine answers NoTransition/AlertAlreadyActive. The partial unique index on
     active alerts is the backstop for two deliveries that overlap rather than follow one another, and
     SaveAsync clears the change tracker on DbUpdateException so MassTransit's retry - which reuses
     the same scope - can actually re-read and succeed.
  3. triggered_at / resolved_at / last_evaluated_at all come from ReadingStored.ObservedAt, never from
     a clock. The engine measures cooldown from the observation's own instant, so a cooldown recorded
     against wall time would drift by exactly the queue's latency.
  4. A NotApplicable decision writes NO alert_rule_state row at all, rather than one saying "not
     breaching": storing a breach flag the engine never computed would fake a transition on the first
     reading that really can be compared.
  5. AlertRuleMapping converts ReadingStored's double to decimal at the boundary and answers null for
     a non-finite value or one outside numeric(12,4)'s range - checked BEFORE the cast, because
     casting a double above decimal.MaxValue throws. Null makes every numeric rule report
     NotApplicable, a far better outcome than faulting the message into the DLQ on arithmetic.
  6. AlertResolved carries the severity the ALERT was raised under, AlertRaised the rule's current
     one. The two differ the moment an operator edits a rule while its alert is open.

Testing - 15 new tests across 5 new files, all against a real PostgreSQL container:
  - AlertEvaluatorTests (11): raise/resolve; per-meter cooldown independence; same-meter cooldown;
    hysteresis holding then clearing with resolved_at/resolved_value; survival of a restart; two
    severities raised by one reading; location scoping; a boolean reading against a numeric rule
    writing neither alert nor state; the Garage motion SEED rule end to end; a disabled rule; and the
    same reading twice raising once. Each uses a metric code no other rule uses
    (AlertingDatabase.NewMetricCode) so the five seed rules - which apply to EVERY location - cannot
    raise alongside it and turn a count assertion into something else; the boolean seed test is the
    deliberate exception.
  - AlertingConsumerTests (3) goes through the REAL broker and the real Program.cs, proving the
    readings.stored binding and the dispatch actually happen: the evaluator tests would pass just as
    happily with the consumer unregistered.
  - Support files: NotificationHost (real Program.cs over WebApplicationFactory, only IAlertDispatcher
    substituted), RecordingAlertDispatcher, StoredReadings. StoredReadings fills previousValue/
    isChanged but no alerting test asserts on them, and that is the point: §6.6's pseudocode derives
    the transition from the event's previous value while this service derives it from
    alert_rule_state, and a test that passed only because the event carried the right previous value
    would not notice the stored flag being ignored.

Two things verification turned up, both fixed here:
  1. NotificationHostTests demanded HTTP 200 from /health/ready on the first call and got 503. Not a
     regression - the correct new behaviour: Program.cs now builds a bus, MassTransit contributes its
     own "ready"-tagged health check, and the bus connects in the BACKGROUND (TASK-012 deliberately
     left WaitUntilStarted off in production, since a service refusing to start over a briefly
     unreachable broker is the worse failure). Readiness is therefore eventually consistent, so the
     test now polls until healthy with a bounded timeout and reports the response body on timeout -
     which is what an orchestrator's probe does anyway, and keeps "the bus was still connecting"
     distinguishable from a real failure in another check. The prior iteration had described this fix
     in its own notes; the edit never reached disk.
  2. AlertingDatabase.NewMetricCode had redundant parentheses around an interpolated string before
     its range index, which StyleCop rejects (SA1119). One-character fix.
  Note AlertingDatabase.NewRule's new hysteresis/cooldown parameters default to AlertRule.Default*
  and NOT to zero, on purpose: AlertRuleConstraintTests asserts the store defaults still apply, and
  assigning the same value keeps TASK-027's EF sentinel behaviour intact.

[2026-09-02] TASK-025 - DONE
Gateway: GraphQL query-depth/complexity limiting, introspection disabled outside Development, and a
schema.graphql snapshot check for drift (PRD's GraphQL hardening pass; persisted queries and the
CI workflow itself are explicitly out of scope here - see below).

Picked up after the previous session's ralph.sh iteration was killed and its nested agent kept
running unsupervised for 8+ minutes past the kill with zero writes reaching disk - the same
orphaned-agent behaviour recorded in TASK-020/029's entries, but this time the agent itself appears
to have stalled rather than lost its shell. The branch existed with an empty working tree; nothing
was lost by starting over.

Before writing any code, verified the exact HotChocolate 16.6.2 API against the real assemblies
(Directory.Packages.props pins this version) with a disposable console app under a temp directory,
rather than guessing from older-version documentation - HotChocolate's schema/execution API has
moved between major versions and gueesing wrong here fails silently at runtime, not at compile time,
since these are all valid method names that just may not exist in the installed version. Confirmed
by building and querying a real minimal schema:
  - `IRequestExecutorBuilder.AddMaxExecutionDepthRule(int)` really rejects a document at validation
    time with a GraphQL error whose extensions carry `allowedExecutionDepth`/`detectedExecutionDepth`
    - HTTP 400, not 200-with-errors.
  - `IRequestExecutorBuilder.DisableIntrospection(bool)` really rejects `__schema`/`__type` with a
    distinct "Introspection is not allowed" error - also HTTP 400.
  - `IRequestExecutorProvider.GetExecutorAsync(name)` (resolvable from `WebApplicationFactory.Services`
    once something forces the host to build) hands back `IRequestExecutor.Schema.ToString()`, which
    IS the SDL - confirmed by actually printing one, not by assuming a HotChocolate v13-era `.Print()`
    extension still exists in v16 (it does not; the type moved and the method is gone).

Implementation (src/Gateway/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api/):
  - GraphQLSecurityLimits.MaxExecutionDepth = 12, a named constant rather than a literal in Program.cs
    - the real domain schema's deepest legitimate selection today is 3 (meters -> currentValues ->
      field), so 12 is headroom for TASK-024/026/032's additions, not a number tuned to the test.
  - Program.cs: `.AddMaxExecutionDepthRule(GraphQLSecurityLimits.MaxExecutionDepth)` with
    `skipIntrospectionFields` left at its default (false) - introspection depth is deliberately
    COUNTED, not exempted, because `__Type.ofType`/`__Field.type` mutually recursing is the classic
    depth-limit bypass a real attacker would reach for; exempting it would have defeated the rule's
    own purpose the moment DisableIntrospection was ever misconfigured or bypassed.
  - `.DisableIntrospection(disable: !allowIntrospection)` where `allowIntrospection` is
    `builder.Environment.IsDevelopment()` captured ONCE at startup - not the IServiceProvider-based
    overload, since the environment cannot change for the life of the process and resolving it per
    request would be indirection with no behavioural difference.
  - `.ModifyRequestOptions(o => o.IncludeExceptionDetails = ...)` from TASK-023 kept as-is; unrelated
    to this task, listed here only because it now sits next to the two new calls in the chain.

Persisted queries were deliberately NOT implemented. The task's acceptance_criteria and test_steps -
the actual testable bar - name only depth-limiting, introspection, and the schema snapshot; the
description's own wording says the GraphQL surface "will be supplemented after TASK-032 adds
subscriptions/alerts," which reads as persisted queries belonging to a later hardening pass once the
schema stops moving. More concretely: HotChocolate's persisted-operations pipeline in "require
persisted" mode rejects any operation that arrives as a raw query string, which is how every existing
Gateway test (and every real ad-hoc GraphiQL session in Development) talks to this API - enabling
enforcement now would need an operation-document store, a build-time hash-extraction step, and a
rewrite of every existing test's request shape, none of which any acceptance criterion here asks for.
Left as a clearly-flagged gap rather than a speculative implementation with zero test coverage.

The schema-drift check is a test, not a CI workflow, because no CI infrastructure exists in this
repository yet - TASK-049 is what adds GitHub Actions workflows at all. SchemaSnapshotTests.
Schema_MatchesTheCommittedSnapshot IS the check the acceptance criterion describes: it resolves the
live IRequestExecutor the same way the running service would, compares its SDL against
src/Gateway/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api/schema.graphql, and fails loudly - with the live SDL written
to a sibling .received file for diffing - on any divergence, committed or not. TASK-049 only has to
point a workflow at `dotnet test gateway.slnf`; the check itself does not need to be reinvented as a
shell script. Locates the repository root via `[CallerFilePath]` on its own source file rather than
a hardcoded path or the test host's working directory, which is what makes it correct unmodified on
a CI runner that checked the repo out somewhere completely different from this machine.

Testing - 4 new tests in tests/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api.Tests/GraphQlHardeningTests.cs, plus the
schema-snapshot test:
  - The depth-limit test exploits `__Type.ofType`/`__Field.type` recursion (ten levels of nested
    `ofType`) rather than adding a recursive field to the domain schema for the sole purpose of
    having something to test against. Run against Development (introspection allowed) specifically
    so a rejection is unambiguously the depth rule and not the separate introspection guard firing
    for an unrelated reason.
  - Introspection tests cover both directions explicitly (refused in "Production", succeeds in
    "Development") rather than only the negative case, since a hardening feature that quietly also
    broke local development would be its own kind of regression.
  - A shallow, ordinary domain query (`meters { currentValues { ... } }`) is asserted to still
    succeed under the same limit, so nothing here proves only "the limit exists" without also proving
    it does not clip real traffic.
  - GatewayApiFactory gained an optional `environment` parameter (default "Development", so every
    test written before this task is unaffected) - the introspection tests are what need "Production".
  - GraphQlClient gained PostExpectingRejectionAsync alongside the existing PostAsync: a
    document-validation failure (depth, introspection) is HTTP 400 in HotChocolate 16, unlike the
    argument-level pageSize>100 validation TASK-023 tested, which answers 200-with-errors - the
    existing PostAsync's EnsureSuccessStatusCode() would have thrown before either new test could
    read the body it actually wants to inspect.

Before writing any HotChocolate subscription code, verified the exact v16.6.2 API with a disposable
console app under a temp directory (same TASK-025 precedent: guessing wrong here fails at runtime, not
compile time) - `ITopicEventReceiver.SubscribeAsync<T>(topic, ct)` returns
`ValueTask<HotChocolate.Execution.ISourceStream<T>>` (NOT `HotChocolate.Subscriptions.ISourceStream`,
a plausible-looking guess that does not exist), `ISourceStream<T>.ReadEventsAsync()` returns
`IAsyncEnumerable<T>`, `ITopicEventSender.SendAsync<T>(topic, payload, ct)`, and critically that this
version has NO `[SubscribeAndResolve]` attribute (removed/renamed since older HotChocolate docs) - the
working pattern is `[Subscribe(With = nameof(SubscribeMethod))]` on the resolver plus a separate method
returning `ValueTask<ISourceStream<T>>`. `AddInMemorySubscriptions()`/`AddSubscriptionType<T>()` are
already reachable with zero new package references (HotChocolate.AspNetCore already transitively
carries HotChocolate.Subscriptions.InMemory).

Subscription design (src/Gateway/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api/GraphQL/):
  - Filtering by (location, meterType) - both optional, null meaning "any" - cannot be a check inside
    the resolver: HotChocolate would still emit one response per event to every subscriber regardless
    of whether the check passed, either a null payload to a non-matching subscriber or the resolver
    silently swallowing events, neither of which is a well-behaved subscription field. Solved instead
    with ReadingStoredTopics.Resolve(location, meterType), ONE method used by both the publishing
    consumer and the subscribing resolver, enumerating the whole (location, meterType) filter space -
    none/location-only/type-only/both - as distinct topic keys. Sending to a topic nobody subscribed to
    is a no-op in HotChocolate's in-memory provider, so the publisher fans out to all four keys
    unconditionally and pays nothing when no subscriber asked for a given combination. Upper-invariant
    on purpose, matching the case-insensitive convention AlertRuleEngine.Matches already set for the
    same two strings.
  - ReadingStoredSubscriptionConsumer: a SECOND, independent MassTransit receive endpoint bound to the
    SAME readings.stored routing key Notification's ReadingStoredConsumer already consumes (TASK-029) -
    a topic exchange delivers a copy to every bound queue it is bound to, so this does not compete with
    Notification for deliveries. Queue name "readings.stored.gateway", distinct from Notification's
    "readings.stored" (ReadingsTopology.StoredQueueName) - reusing the same queue name would have both
    services competing for one copy of each message instead of each getting their own. Converts the
    wire double (Contracts.MetricValue) to a nullable decimal the same range-checked-before-cast way
    AlertRuleMapping does (casting a double outside decimal's range throws OverflowException, and a
    poll result should never fault this consumer), but does NOT round to 4 decimal places the way that
    helper does - this is a live streamed value, not something written into a numeric(12,4) column.
  - ReadingStoredPayload deliberately shaped like ReadingReadModel (Location/MeterType/MetricCode/
    ValueNumeric/ValueBool/IsChanged/ObservedAt) rather than re-exposing Contracts.ReadingStored
    directly, so a client already familiar with the `readings` query recognises the fields on
    `onReadingStored` too.
  - Alert events (AlertRaised/AlertResolved) are NOT on this subscription and never will be through
    this seam - IAlertDispatcher's own doc comment (TASK-029) already says they stay in-process for
    Notification's own SignalR hub (TASK-031, still pending); onReadingStored streams readings only,
    per PRD F7's table.

Alerts/alertRules read side (mirrors the existing GatewayReadDbContext/IGatewayReadContext pattern for
a SECOND owning service's schema):
  - New IGatewayAlertingReadContext (Gateway.Application/Alerting) + GatewayAlertingReadDbContext/
    GatewayAlertingReadContext (Gateway.Infrastructure), reading `alerts`/`alert_rules` off the SAME
    `weakapphandler` database and the SAME "Gateway" connection string as the core-schema context - one
    physical database, two DbContexts, because the tables belong to a different owning service
    (Notification), not because the connection differs. Own AlertStatus/AlertSeverity/AlertOperator
    enums and AlertEntity/AlertRuleEntity classes, not references to Notification.Api.Domain - same
    "no compile-time dependency on the schema owner" rule MeterEntity already follows for Processor's
    tables. Own converters, storing the exact same lower-case wire codes Notification's converters
    write (`active`/`resolved`, `info`/`warning`/`critical`, `gt`/`gte`/`lt`/`lte`/`eq`) - same column,
    not a copy of it, so the two spellings must never diverge.
  - REAL BUG CAUGHT BEFORE IT SHIPPED: GatewayReadDbContext.OnModelCreating originally called
    `ApplyConfigurationsFromAssembly(typeof(GatewayReadDbContext).Assembly)`, and the new alerting
    configurations landed in the SAME assembly (Gateway.Infrastructure). ApplyConfigurationsFromAssembly
    is not aware of which DbContext invoked it - it just finds every IEntityTypeConfiguration<T> in the
    assembly and applies it to whatever ModelBuilder was handed to it. Left as-is, GatewayReadDbContext's
    model would have silently gained AlertEntity/AlertRuleEntity (and vice versa for
    GatewayAlertingReadDbContext gaining Meter/Reading/MeterCurrentState) the moment both configuration
    sets existed in one assembly. Fixed by switching BOTH contexts from the assembly-wide scan to
    explicit `modelBuilder.ApplyConfiguration(new XConfiguration())` calls, one per entity that context
    actually owns - a few more lines, but immune to this class of cross-contamination regardless of what
    else lands in the assembly later.
  - Query.cs gained `alerts` (paginated, [UseFiltering]/[UseSorting] over Status/Severity/Location/
    TriggeredAt satisfies "filter by status/severity/location/time" the same generic way TASK-023's
    readings query already satisfies its own filter list) and `alertRules` (unpaginated list, same
    reasoning as `meters`). Default sort on `alerts` is TriggeredAt descending, matching the DB index
    direction and PRD §6.8's reverse-chronological feed.

Deploy note, NOT acted on: deploy/postgres/init/01-create-service-roles.sql's `gateway` role grant is
`GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON DATABASE weakapphandler` + `GRANT ALL ON SCHEMA public` - neither actually
grants table-level SELECT on tables owned by a different role (processor/notification) in real
PostgreSQL, so a genuine production deployment likely cannot yet read cross-service tables at all. This
is a PRE-EXISTING gap (the same grant already claims to let `gateway` read Processor's `meters`/
`readings`, unverified until TASK-043's real least-privilege pass), not something this task introduced
or could fix without doing TASK-043's job early - flagging it here since alerts/alert_rules make the
gap concrete for a second table set. Tests never hit it: both GatewayReadDbContext and
GatewayAlertingReadDbContext connect through the Testcontainers fixture's superuser connection, not
through the `gateway`/`notification` role split at all.

session, confirmed via `docker info` before committing to this task):
  - AlertsQueryTests (3): filter by location (newest-first ordering asserted explicitly), filter by
    status=ACTIVE excluding a resolved alert, filter by severity+triggeredAt range. Each test's rule
    uses NotificationSchemaSeed.NewMetricCode() (a code no seed rule uses) so the five §6.6 seed rules
    TASK-027's migration always applies cannot leak into a count assertion.
  - AlertRulesQueryTests (2): filter by this test's own metric code returns exactly one row with every
    field correctly mapped/converted; unfiltered query asserts >= 6 (five seed rules + this test's own)
    rather than an exact count, since the seed rules are always present in the shared table.
  - ReadingStoredSubscriptionTests (2): goes through the DI-resolved ITopicEventReceiver/ITopicEventSender
    directly rather than a graphql-ws client - the WebSocket transport is HotChocolate's own tested
    code, not something this task adds, and driving the subscription through the exact seam
    Subscription.SubscribeToOnReadingStoredAsync itself uses is a more direct proof of the routing logic
    than a transport round trip would be. First test: no filter arguments, receives a published
    ReadingStored with all fields round-tripped correctly (including the double->decimal conversion).
    Second test: subscribes to Kitchen/air_quality specifically, publishes a Garage/air_quality reading
    FIRST then a Kitchen/air_quality one, asserts the stream's first (and only) event is the Kitchen one
    - proving the non-matching event was never routed there at all, not read-and-discarded. Each test
    gets its own RabbitMQ virtual host (AlertingConsumerTests' precedent) so concurrent tests cannot
    collide on the fixed "readings.stored.gateway" queue name.
  - NotificationSchemaSeed: seeds alert_rules/alerts via Notification's own AlertingDbContext (new
    ProjectReference from Gateway.Api.Tests to Notification.Api, test-only, added to gateway.slnf) so
    these tests exercise the Gateway against exactly the tables/columns production writes. The
    reference is ALIASED (`Aliases="NotificationApi"` in the csproj) because both Gateway.Api and
    Notification.Api declare their own top-level `Program` type for WebApplicationFactory, and an
    unaliased second reference makes every unqualified `Program` in the test project ambiguous (CS0433)
    the moment both assemblies are on the reference list at once. NotificationSchemaSeed.cs is the only
    file with `extern alias NotificationApi;` - every public member takes/returns only Guid/string, so
    the ceremony does not spread to the actual test classes.
  - REAL BUG CAUGHT BY THIS: adding MassTransit to Gateway.Api's Program.cs (unconditionally, for the
    new receive endpoint) meant every EXISTING Gateway test (Meters/Readings/GraphQlHardening/
    SchemaSnapshot, none of which previously configured RabbitMq at all) would now start a background
    bus connection attempt against the default localhost:5672/guest/guest the moment the host boots -
    either hanging quietly against nothing, or, worse, silently binding a real queue on a developer's
    own `docker compose up` broker if one happened to be running locally. Fixed by making
    GatewayApiFactory.Create require a `rabbitMqConnectionString` parameter (mirroring
    NotificationHost's pattern) and updating all 9 pre-existing call sites to pass
    fixture.RabbitMq.ConnectionString - every Gateway test now points at the same real, ephemeral
    Testcontainers broker the collection already spins up, never at a guessed default.

Branch `task/task-031-signalr-alerts-hub` created from an up-to-date `main` (confirmed via `git

the ONLY shell commands that worked this session). Researched the existing seam first (an Explore
agent, since the PowerShell tool was still working for git at that point): `IAlertDispatcher`
(src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api/Alerting/IAlertDispatcher.cs) is a two-method
interface (`DispatchRaisedAsync`/`DispatchResolvedAsync`) called once, in-process, after an alert
commits; `LoggingAlertDispatcher` is registered via `TryAddSingleton` in `AddAlerting`
(AlertingServiceCollectionExtensions.cs) specifically so a host - or this task - can register its own
implementation first and keep the default out of the way.

Implementation (all under src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api/):
  - RealTime/AlertsHub.cs - `[Authorize(Policy = ServicePolicies.Viewer)]` empty `Hub` (push-only, no
    client-invokable methods). Viewer policy, not Admin, since any authenticated user should see
    alerts, not just operators. The XML doc records the single-replica-only caveat PRD §6.7 calls out
    explicitly (no SignalR backplane configured).
  - RealTime/SignalRAlertDispatcher.cs - `IAlertDispatcher` over `IHubContext<AlertsHub>`, broadcasting
    to `Clients.All` under method names "AlertRaised"/"AlertResolved" with the Contracts records
    themselves as the payload (no separate DTO - a client already needs to know AlertRaised/
    AlertResolved's shape from the REST/GraphQL side eventually, so there is nothing to gain from a
    third shape). A broadcast failure is caught (with `#pragma warning disable CA1031`, the same
    pattern IngestionWorker.cs already uses for "the loop must survive every failure") and logged
    rather than rethrown, EXCEPT a cancellation tied to the caller's own token, which is allowed to
    propagate - per IAlertDispatcher's own doc comment, a dispatch failure must not fault the
    already-committed alert's RabbitMQ message and send it back through the endpoint's retry policy.
  - Program.cs: `AddSignalR()` and `AddSingleton<IAlertDispatcher, SignalRAlertDispatcher>()` added
    BEFORE `AddNotificationPersistence`/`AddAlerting`, so `AddAlerting`'s `TryAddSingleton` sees an
    existing registration and never adds `LoggingAlertDispatcher` in production. `MapHub<AlertsHub>
    ("/hubs/alerts")` added next to `MapControllers()`. (NotificationHost.cs, the existing test host
    used by AlertingConsumerTests/NotificationHostTests, still wins over this: its own
    `ConfigureServices` appends ANOTHER `AddSingleton<IAlertDispatcher>(recordingDispatcher)` AFTER
    Program.cs runs, and DI resolves the last registration - confirmed by reading, not by running,
    which is exactly the gap the blocker below leaves open.)
  - appsettings.json: added an `Auth` section (Issuer/Audience/JwksUri/RequireHttpsMetadata) copied
    from Ingestor's own appsettings.json pattern (TASK-017) - Notification had NO Auth config at all
    before this task, because it had no protected endpoints yet. Same port 5091 (Auth Service's local
    profile), same "Authority stays unset, JWKS pinned directly" rationale.

Testing (tests/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api.Tests/), matching TASK-031's three test_steps exactly:
  - AlertsHubHost.cs - a new test host, modeled directly on IngestorAdminHost.cs (TASK-017's admin API
    tests): hosts the REAL Auth Service and the REAL Notification service together, wires
    Notification's JWKS HTTP client to the Auth host's TestServer handler (`ConfigurePrimaryHttpMessageHandler
    (authFactory.Server.CreateHandler)`), and mints a real viewer token via Auth's own `/login`.
    Deliberately does NOT override `IAlertDispatcher` the way `NotificationHost.cs` does - the whole
    point is to exercise the real `SignalRAlertDispatcher`. Exposes `CreateHubConnection(accessToken)`
    building a `Microsoft.AspNetCore.SignalR.Client.HubConnection` against the Notification TestServer,
    forced onto `HttpTransportType.LongPolling` (TestServer has no real socket for WebSockets to
    upgrade over - a well-known constraint of testing SignalR through WebApplicationFactory, not

  - Had to alias the new `ProjectReference` to `WeakAppHandler.Auth` (`Aliases="AuthApi"` in the
    .csproj) and add `extern alias AuthApi;` to AlertsHubHost.cs, exactly the fix TASK-032's own entry
    above documents for the identical problem in Gateway.Api.Tests: both Notification.Api and Auth
    declare their own top-level global `Program` class, and an unaliased second reference makes every
    unqualified `Program` already in this test project (NotificationHost.cs, NotificationHostTests.cs)
    ambiguous (CS0433) the moment both assemblies are referenced at once. IngestorAdminHost.cs sidesteps
    the same problem differently (using `IngestionPoller`, not `Program`, as the Ingestor's

    types (JwtTokenService/AuthDbContext/AuthSeedData) it never needed a `WebApplicationFactory` marker
    workaround for on the Ingestor side. Only `AlertsHubHost.cs` carries the `extern alias`; every other
    file in the project (including the new AlertsHubTests.cs) uses the unqualified, already-familiar
    `WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api.*` types.
  - AlertsHubTests.cs - 4 tests: (1) connecting without an access token throws (the exact SignalR
    client exception for a rejected handshake is transport-dependent, so the assertion is deliberately
    `Assert.ThrowsAnyAsync<Exception>` rather than pinned to one type); (2) a connected client with a
    real viewer token receives "AlertRaised" for a breaching reading published through the real
    consumer pipeline, asserting the same fields AlertingConsumerTests already asserts on the
    dispatcher-level event; (3) a subsequent below-threshold reading produces a separate "AlertResolved"
    for the same AlertId; (4) exactly one "AlertRaised" arrives for one breaching reading (a 2-second
    grace period after the first receipt, checking no duplicate shows up) - the acceptance criterion
    "the same event is not sent twice to the same client." Each test gets its own RabbitMQ virtual host,
    the AlertingConsumerTests precedent, so concurrent runs can't cross-deliver on the fixed queue name.

    notification.slnf`/`dotnet test notification.slnf` pull in the new test-only dependency.

[2026-09-02] TASK-024 - DONE
Gateway: aggregations query bucketing readings via SQL date_trunc/generate_series, grouped by
location/meterType/metric, with every bucket in range present even when empty (PRD F4). This clears
the last unblocked critical/high-priority task in the backlog.
