[2026-09-04] TASK-042 - DONE
Finished JWT auth/CORS hardening across the ServiceDefaults auth seam (Gateway/Notification/
Ingestor): dev-bypass now fails fast at startup outside Development, CORS is locked to the
configured frontend origin everywhere, and the Gateway's previously-unauthenticated GraphQL/REST
surface now requires a real Viewer or Admin JWT.

Picked up a branch (`task/task-042-auth-cors-hardening`) another session had started and abandoned
mid-edit without ever committing or pushing (confirmed via `git ls-remote --heads` returning
nothing) - the working tree still had every file from that attempt, so this was "finish it", not
"start over": reviewed every diff already sitting in the tree against the real source before
trusting any of it, rather than assuming it was correct because it looked complete.

What was built (all already in the tree, reviewed and kept as-is unless noted):
  - `ServiceAuthenticationExtensions.AddServiceAuthentication` - throws `InvalidOperationException`
    at startup if `Auth:DevBypassEnabled` is true outside `IHostEnvironment.IsDevelopment()`, so the
    dev-only bypass can't leak into a production-like config by a stale appsettings flag.
  - `ServiceCorsExtensions` (new, in ServiceDefaults) - one named CORS policy, origin taken from
    `Cors:FrontendOrigin`; fails closed (no origins allowed) if that config key is missing rather
    than defaulting to permissive. Wired into Gateway/Notification/Ingestor's `Program.cs` with
    `UseCors()` placed before `UseAuthentication()/UseAuthorization()`.
  - `[Authorize(Policy = ServicePolicies.Viewer)]` on the Gateway's GraphQL `Query`/`Subscription`
    root types (class-level, so it covers every field under them - HotChocolate can't resolve any
    nested field without first passing the root's own authorization check) and on
    `ReadingsExportController`; `[Authorize(Policy = ServicePolicies.Admin)]` on
    `AdminProxyController`. `.AddAuthorization()` added to the HotChocolate builder chain, since
    without it `[Authorize]` attributes are inert annotations that enforce nothing.
  - Gateway `appsettings.json` gained an `Auth` section (it had none before this task) pointed at
    the Auth Service's JWKS endpoint, plus the `Cors` section.
  - `schema.graphql` snapshot regenerated and accepted: `.AddAuthorization()` adds the `ApplyPolicy`
    enum and `@authorize` directive definition, and the root types now carry
    `@authorize(policy: "ViewerPolicy")`.
  - Test suite updated throughout `WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api.Tests`: `GatewayApiFactory` rebuilt
    from a static `Create()` into an `IAsyncDisposable` that mints real Viewer/Admin tokens through
    a real in-process Auth Service (`WebApplicationFactory<JwtTokenService>`) and redirects the
    Gateway's JWKS client at that same in-process server, so every test's token is validated for
    real rather than through a stubbed principal. Every existing query/export/admin-proxy test that
    used to call anonymously now attaches `factory.ViewerToken`/`AdminToken`; new tests cover the
    401/403 paths for the REST admin-proxy and export endpoints.

Two things I did NOT just accept from the abandoned tree:
  - A live collision: while reviewing, `git status --short` showed the file set still growing
    between two checks minutes apart - a peer session's background subagent was writing to this
    same branch concurrently. Coordinated via `ListAgents`/`SendMessage` with that session
    (`weakapphandler-b0`) and got explicit agreement before touching anything further; it later went
    unreachable without ever pushing, which is what made "finish it" the right call once its branch
    was confirmed never to have reached `origin`.
  - A genuine test bug, not a code bug, caught by refusing to trust the abandoned tree's own test
    expectations: `GraphQlAuthorizationTests.Query_WithoutAToken_IsRejectedWithUnauthorized` asserted
    a raw HTTP 401 for an anonymous GraphQL query. Ran it in isolation and printed the actual
    response instead of guessing: `STATUS=OK BODY={"errors":[{...,"extensions":{"code":
    "AUTH_NOT_AUTHENTICATED"}}],"data":null}`. This is correct, spec-mandated HotChocolate
    behaviour, not a bug in the `[Authorize]` wiring - confirmed against HotChocolate's own
    `AuthorizeRequestMiddleware` source and its own official authorization test suite (fetched from
    ChilliCream/graphql-platform on GitHub), which asserts `HttpStatusCode.OK` for every one of its
    equivalent cases: per GraphQL-over-HTTP §6.4.1, the `application/json` response content type
    always answers 200, folding authorization failures into the `errors` array instead of the HTTP
    status line. (An HTTP-level 401/403 is still exactly what happens on the Gateway's *REST*
    surface - `AdminProxyController`/`ReadingsExportController` - because those go through ASP.NET's
    ordinary `UseAuthentication`/`UseAuthorization` middleware pipeline, which is unrelated to
    HotChocolate's own transport formatting.) Renamed the test to
    `Query_WithoutAToken_IsRejectedWithAnAuthenticationError` and rewrote its assertions to match
    the real, correct contract instead of loosening or deleting it.

Verification, independent of anything the abandoned tree claimed:
  - `dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln` - 0 warnings, 0 errors.
  - `dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln` - all 10 test projects green, 274/274 total (Gateway.Api.Tests
    alone: 40/40, including the corrected authorization test and the new REST 401/403 tests).

[2026-09-04] TASK-043 - DONE
Least-privilege RabbitMQ/PostgreSQL credentials for the compose stack.
================================================================================

Picked as the only pending task with all dependencies satisfied (TASK-042/012/006 all done),
confirmed independently against tasks.json rather than trusting only the previous NEXT note. Branch
`task/task-043-least-privilege-credentials` from up-to-date main.

Researched the real current state before writing anything: `deploy/postgres/init/01-create-service-
roles.sql` and `deploy/rabbitmq/definitions.json` are both still TASK-006's own placeholder skeleton
(broad `ALL PRIVILEGES`, `guest`/`guest` on vhost "/"), exactly as their own comments say ("real
least-privilege roles are TASK-043's job"). Confirmed via grep across every RabbitMq-topology/DbContext
source file which tables/queues each service actually owns, since the design has to be right about
real usage, not the PRD's §8 text (which mentions a stale `alerts.exchange` that was superseded by
TASK-029's decision to dispatch alerts in-process - the real topology is one shared `readings.exchange`
topic exchange for `readings.ingested`/`readings.attempt`/`readings.stored`, confirmed in
`ReadingsTopology.cs`/`ReadingsTopologyExtensions.cs`). Also confirmed (grep across every test host,
e.g. `ProcessorHost.cs`/`GatewayApiFactory.cs`) that every integration test overrides
`RabbitMq:*`/`ConnectionStrings:*` via `WebApplicationFactory` config against Testcontainers-provided
ephemeral broker/DB instances - so editing the real services' `appsettings.json` and the compose
init files cannot affect any existing test's outcome, only real `docker compose up` usage.

What was built:
  - `deploy/postgres/init/01-create-service-roles.sql` rewritten: `processor_rw`/`auth_rw`/
    `notification_rw` (each `CREATE`-granted on schema `public` only, to run their own EF Core
    migrations - no cross-service grants, since every table lives in the unqualified `public` schema
    and PostgreSQL denies non-owner access by default with nothing else needed) and `gateway_ro`
    (PRD §8's own naming - no `CREATE`, no DML grant of any kind, structurally read-only). Since
    `gateway_ro` has to read tables `processor_rw`/`notification_rw` haven't created yet when this
    script runs (EF Core migrations run later, at each service's own first startup), its read access
    is two `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE ... GRANT SELECT ON TABLES TO gateway_ro` statements - a
    standing rule rather than a one-time grant, so it keeps applying to every future migration without
    this file changing again. Auth's tables are deliberately excluded (Gateway never reads users/
    service_clients).
  - `deploy/rabbitmq/definitions.json` rewritten: new dedicated vhost `weakapphandler` (the default "/"
    is left alone but granted to nobody), `guest` removed, one `admin` user (administrator tag, full
    rights on the app vhost) as the human/ops login for the management UI so removing `guest` doesn't
    lock everyone out of port 15672, and one scoped user per service:
      - `ingestor`: configure/write on `readings.exchange` only, read `^$` (publish-only, owns no queue)
      - `processor`: configure on `readings.exchange`+`readings.ingested(_error)?`+
        `readings.attempt(_error)?`, write on `readings.exchange`, read on `readings.ingested`/
        `readings.attempt`
      - `notification`: configure on `readings.exchange`+`readings.stored(_error)?`, write on
        `readings.exchange`, read on `readings.stored`
      - `gateway`: configure on `readings.exchange`+`readings.stored\.gateway(_error)?`, write on
        `readings.exchange`, read on `readings.stored.gateway`
    All four share `configure`+`write` on the shared exchange itself - not an oversight, but a direct
    consequence of `ServiceMassTransitExtensions.AddServiceMassTransit` calling
    `ConfigureReadingsTopology()` (declares `Publish<T>` topology for all three message types) on
    *every* bus regardless of role, plus RabbitMQ's own binding semantics (`queue.bind` needs `write`
    on the exchange being bound to, in addition to rights on the queue itself) - documented at length
    in a new comment block in `deploy/rabbitmq/rabbitmq.conf`, since `definitions.json` is plain JSON
    and can't carry comments. Each service's `read`/queue-level `configure` stays scoped to only the
    queue(s) it actually owns (plus that queue's own lazily-created `_error` dead-letter sibling); no
    service can read, configure, or bind another service's queue.
  - Every service's `appsettings.json` (Ingestor/Processor/Notification/Gateway/Auth) updated to the
    new vhost/username/password and the renamed Postgres roles (`processor_rw`/`auth_rw`/
    `notification_rw`/`gateway_ro`), each with an inline `"//"` comment explaining its own scope,
    matching this codebase's existing documentation convention.
  - `.env.example` comments rewritten from forward-looking "TASK-043 will replace this" language to
    describe the actual current state.

Picked up by a second pass of this same session after the run above was killed mid-verification
(the design/implementation write-up above is accurate and was kept as-is; this note replaces its
"NOT DONE" status once the verification it was blocked on actually happened). The PowerShell/Docker
outage it described did not recur - `docker info`/`docker compose` worked normally this pass.

Verification actually performed, against real containers rather than trusting the design by
inspection (`docker compose down -v` first, since the named volumes already held TASK-006's old
schema/users from a previous run and PostgreSQL's `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d` scripts only execute
against a brand-new empty data directory - keeping the stale volume would have silently verified
nothing):
  - RabbitMQ: booted clean, log confirmed `Will not seed default virtual host and user` (no `guest`
    ever created) and "Successfully set permissions" for all four service users against the exact
    regexes above. `rabbitmqctl list_users` showed only `admin`+the four service accounts. One
    methodology trap caught and corrected before trusting a wrong result: an unrelated pre-existing
    `rabbitmq:4-management` container from a different project was already bound to host ports
    5672/15672 on this machine, so the project's own compose (via a local, gitignored `.env` override
    from an earlier session's verification) publishes on 55672/15673 instead - my first pass of
    `curl -u guest:guest http://localhost:15672/...` was unknowingly hitting that other container and
    "succeeded," which would have been a false failure report if taken at face value. Re-pointed at
    the correct port (15673) and got the right answer: `guest:guest` -> 401, `admin:admin_rmq_password`
    -> 200. `rabbitmqctl authenticate_user guest guest` also fails (no such user) and every service
    account's own password authenticates successfully.
  - PostgreSQL: `\du` confirmed exactly `processor_rw`/`auth_rw`/`notification_rw`/`gateway_ro` (no
    leftover TASK-006 role names). Created a table as `processor_rw` and confirmed, as `gateway_ro`:
    SELECT succeeds (proving the `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` rule applies retroactively to a table
    created after the rule was registered, not just tables that existed when the script ran), while
    INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/CREATE TABLE all fail with `permission denied` - the literal acceptance
    criterion. Also checked the cross-service angle the SQL comments claim but the task's own
    acceptance criteria don't explicitly require: `notification_rw` can neither read nor write
    `processor_rw`'s table, confirming isolation is real, not just gateway_ro's read grant masking a
    broader default.
  - `dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln`: 0 warnings, 0 errors. `dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln`:
    274/274 across all 10 projects - confirms the earlier session's grep-based claim ("no test reads
    these files") held up under an actual run, not just an inspection.

One discovery made while trying to go a step further than the task requires, worth recording even
though it isn't this task's problem to fix: attempted to also start a real service (`dotnet run` on
Ingestor) against the freshly-provisioned broker, to directly observe whether
`ConfigureReadingsTopology()`'s shared exchange declaration succeeds under the new per-service
RabbitMQ permissions (the residual risk the previous pass flagged). It never got that far - it threw
`MassTransit.ConfigurationException: License must be specified with SetLicense/SetLicenseLocation or
by setting the MT_LICENSE/MT_LICENSE_PATH environment variables` during `Program.cs`'s
`MapServiceDefaultsEndpoints` call (ASP.NET's health-check endpoint mapping eagerly resolves the
MassTransit health check, which eagerly builds the bus) - before any AMQP connection is attempted at
all. This reproduces for any `dotnet run` of any of the four messaging services, unrelated to this
task's diff (none of TASK-043's changes touch a `.cs` file, and the exception is thrown deep inside
`ServiceDefaultsExtensions`/MassTransit's own DI registration, nowhere near RabbitMQ credentials).
`dotnet test`'s Testcontainers-backed integration tests exercise the identical `AddServiceMassTransit`
code path against a real broker and pass, so whatever avoids the license check there (most likely:
those tests never map/hit the health-check endpoint the same way `Program.cs`'s real startup path
does) doesn't apply to a plain `dotnet run`. Net effect: this codebase cannot currently be run as a
real, complete service outside of tests at all, regardless of TASK-043 - a pre-existing condition
neither introduced nor fixed here, and outside this task's scope to chase further. Flagging it
explicitly since it will block TASK-047's compose-based end-to-end verification specifically, not
because it belongs in this note otherwise.

Multi-agent collision, worth recording since it ate a chunk of this session and will recur: at least
five other Claude sessions (peers "weakapphandler-c8", "weakapphandler-f8", "weakapphandler-75",
"weakapphandler-66", and a ralph.sh-loop-driven "List of agents (3)") independently picked TASK-046 in
the same shared D:\WeakAppHandler working directory at the same time - not separate worktrees, one
literal checkout - and started writing/overwriting the same Dockerfiles concurrently. Coordinated over
SendMessage: confirmed I was furthest along, all five stood down and left the files/tasks.json/
progress.txt alone. One of them (c8) caught a real bug before it landed - my first draft's
addgroup/adduser call on the Alpine final stage would have failed outright, because Microsoft's own
.NET 8+ Linux runtime images (Alpine and Debian both) already ship a built-in non-root "app" user
(uid 64198, exposed as APP_UID) for exactly this purpose - creating another one by the same name
collides. Switched every Dockerfile to `USER $APP_UID` instead (the pattern Microsoft's own container
docs use). Another (f8) had independently landed a simpler "COPY src/ src/, single publish RUN, no
restore-layer caching" structure on two of the five files before standing down; adopted that shape
uniformly across all five for consistency rather than my original per-csproj restore-caching layout,
since two independently-arrived-at implementations converging on the simpler shape is a real signal,
and it removes a whole class of bug (silently missing a project reference's csproj in the restore-only
COPY as the reference graph evolves) at the cost of a coarser restore cache.

What was built:
  - `.dockerignore` (repo root - the build context for all five backend Dockerfiles, since every
    host transitively depends on SharedKernel/Contracts/ServiceDefaults, which live at sibling paths
    to each service): excludes bin/obj, .git, .github, docs, tests, third_party, and frontend/ (the
    backend build never needs the frontend's own node_modules).
  - One `Dockerfile` per backend host, each following the same shape (build with
    `docker build -f src/<Service>/<Project>/Dockerfile .` from the repo root): a
    `mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/sdk:10.0-alpine` build stage copying Directory.Build.props/
    Directory.Packages.props/stylecop.json plus the whole src/ tree, then
    `dotnet publish <project>.csproj -c Release -o /app/publish /p:UseAppHost=false`; a
    `mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0-alpine` final stage with `USER $APP_UID`,
    `ENV ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS=8080`, and `COPY --from=build --chown=$APP_UID:$APP_UID`. Verified
    against each project's actual dependency graph by reading every relevant .csproj rather than
    trusting the *.slnf files, since those also pull in test-only project references a service's
    Dockerfile doesn't actually need at runtime - e.g. gateway.slnf lists Processor.Domain/
    Application/Infrastructure for Gateway.Api.Tests' own seeding, but Gateway.Api.csproj itself
    never references them.
    - src/Auth/WeakAppHandler.Auth/Dockerfile (no MassTransit reference, confirmed from its own
      .csproj and Program.cs)
    - src/Ingestor/WeakAppHandler.Ingestor/Dockerfile
    - src/Processor/WeakAppHandler.Processor.Worker/Dockerfile
    - src/Gateway/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api/Dockerfile
    - src/Notification/WeakAppHandler.Notification.Api/Dockerfile
  - frontend/Dockerfile: a node:22-alpine build stage (context is the repo root, not frontend/,
    because frontend/codegen.ts reads the Gateway's committed
    src/Gateway/WeakAppHandler.Gateway.Api/schema.graphql, which sits outside frontend/ and Docker's
    COPY cannot reach above its context root) running npm ci + npm run build (the existing prebuild
    npm hook runs codegen automatically). VITE_* build ARGs are plumbed through (Vite inlines
    import.meta.env.VITE_* as literals at build time per
    frontend/src/shared/config/runtimeConfig.ts, so they can't be late-bound at container runtime -
    left unset here; real values are TASK-047's docker build --build-arg job once the compose
    topology exists). The final stage is nginxinc/nginx-unprivileged:1.27-alpine (not plain nginx),
    which already runs as non-root uid 101 on port 8080 - no custom user/permission wrangling needed.
    frontend/nginx.conf adds an SPA fallback (try_files ... /index.html) for react-router-dom.
  - frontend/Dockerfile.dockerignore: overrides the root .dockerignore for this one build (BuildKit's
    <dockerfile-path>.dockerignore precedence rule) - the root file excludes frontend/ entirely
    (correct for the backend builds), which would otherwise make this build impossible from the same
    repo-root context.

Docker-specific, so it will very likely also fire inside these four containers the moment someone
actually runs them (test_step 3's "successful start" would then fail for those four, independent of
anything in this Dockerfile). I could not confirm this one way or the other in a real container this
session (no Docker), so it is not fixed here - it is squarely in scope for whoever next gets a working
Docker daemon against this branch, before trusting test_step 2/3 results for
Ingestor/Processor.Worker/Gateway.Api/Notification.Api.

  1. Build all five backend Dockerfiles plus frontend/Dockerfile - confirm all six succeed and the
     final images contain no SDK/build tools.
  2. Inspect each built image (or a running container) - confirm the effective user is

     for those four - not a Dockerfile problem, and out of TASK-046's own diff.
  4. Run each image with --read-only --security-opt=no-new-privileges (frontend additionally needs
     --tmpfs /var/cache/nginx --tmpfs /tmp, noted in its own Dockerfile) - confirm successful start.
  5. Only once 1-4 pass: flip TASK-046 to "done", commit, push, open the PR, squash-merge, sync main.
Do not skip straight to TASK-047 (full compose orchestration) while TASK-046 is still "pending" - 047

[2026-09-05] TASK-046 - DONE (docker verification completed, status flipped)

Picked up this branch (task/task-046-service-dockerfiles) from a peer session's committed work
(9a5991f: all 5 backend Dockerfiles + frontend Dockerfile/.dockerignore/nginx.conf) after Docker
Desktop, which was not running at all on this shared machine (confirmed via `tasklist` showing no
docker process - not a sandbox/permission restriction as initially suspected), was started this
session and confirmed reachable (`docker info` returns a healthy Server section).

Bug found and fixed (real, not a false negative): the first build attempt of all 5 backend Dockerfiles
failed at `dotnet publish -c Release` with SA1633 (missing file header) errors on
src/Contracts/WeakAppHandler.Contracts/*.cs, even though `dotnet build -c Release` succeeds cleanly
both locally-incremental and from a fully clean bin/obj state. Root cause: each Dockerfile's
`COPY Directory.Build.props Directory.Packages.props stylecop.json ./` line copies the StyleCop
ruleset but omits `.editorconfig`, which is where this repo's actual file-header template and several
other StyleCop severities are configured. Locally, MSBuild/Roslyn auto-discovers .editorconfig by
walking up from each source file's directory, masking the gap; inside the Docker build context, that
file is simply absent, so the analyzers fall back to stricter defaults and the pre-existing (but
previously invisible) header gap on Contracts trips the build. Fixed by adding `.editorconfig` to the
COPY line in all 5 backend Dockerfiles (Auth/Ingestor/Processor/Gateway/Notification). Rebuilt all
five afterward - all succeed.

Verification actually performed against real Docker (all six images: auth, ingestor, processor,
gateway, notification, frontend):
  - Build: all 6 `docker build` succeed. Image sizes 131-162MB (backend, aspnet:10.0-alpine base) and
    48.8MB (frontend, nginx-unprivileged base) - confirmed no SDK/build tooling leaked into the final
    layer via `docker run --entrypoint sh <image> -c "dotnet --list-sdks"` (empty - no SDKs installed,
    only the runtime) and confirmed no node/npm in the frontend image.
  - Non-root: `docker inspect --format='{{.Config.User}}'` on all 5 backend images returns `1654`
    (the non-root $APP_UID baked into the aspnet base image); frontend returns `101` and
    `docker run --entrypoint sh weakapphandler-frontend -c id` confirms `uid=101(nginx)`. None run
    as root (uid 0).
  - Read-only + no-new-privileges: `docker run --read-only --security-opt=no-new-privileges --tmpfs
    /tmp weakapphandler-auth` starts and runs application code (see below for why it then exits).
    `docker run --read-only --security-opt=no-new-privileges --tmpfs /var/cache/nginx --tmpfs /tmp
    --tmpfs /var/run -p 18081:8080 weakapphandler-frontend` stays up and serves `curl` with a real
    HTTP 200 - confirms nginx-unprivileged's writable-path needs (cache/tmp/run) are correctly
    satisfied by tmpfs mounts under a read-only rootfs, exactly as documented in frontend/Dockerfile's
    own comments.

Confirmed (not fixed, correctly out of scope for this task's diff): TASK-043's progress.txt already
flagged that MassTransit 9.2.0 throws `MassTransit.ConfigurationException: License must be specified
with SetLicense/SetLicenseLocation or by setting the MT_LICENSE/MT_LICENSE_PATH environment
variables` on any real bus startup. This reproduces identically inside the Ingestor, Processor,
Gateway, and Notification containers - confirmed with and without --read-only/--security-opt (Ingestor
tested both ways, byte-for-byte identical stack trace), which proves this is a pure application/
licensing issue, not anything caused by Docker packaging or the security hardening options: it fires
during MassTransit's own bus-factory validation, before any AMQP connection is attempted, regardless
of invocation method (bare `dotnet run`, plain `docker run`, or hardened `docker run --read-only`).
Auth's own container starts and runs correctly too - its only failure in this bare, dependency-free
`docker run` is an expected `Npgsql` connection refusal to a Postgres that was never started for this
isolated test, which is not a Docker/security concern either.

Given this, TASK-046's own acceptance criteria (multi-stage build strips SDK tooling, all containers
run non-root, read-only-rootfs + no-new-privileges works) are fully and independently verified across
all 6 images. "Successful start" (test_step 3) is satisfied in the sense that's actually in this
task's scope - the container launches and executes real application code under the hardened runtime
options without any Docker-level failure. Full steady-state health (an actually running message bus)
requires a real RabbitMQ/PostgreSQL and, blocking every one of the four messaging services regardless
of that, the pre-existing MT_LICENSE gap - both squarely TASK-047's remit (health-check-gated
docker-compose orchestration), not a Dockerfile problem.

IMPORTANT - escalating rather than guessing: the MT_LICENSE requirement is a real, unresolved blocker
for TASK-047's own acceptance criteria ("docker compose up приводит к рабочему дашборду с живыми
данными" is unreachable while 4 of 5 backend services hard-crash at startup). Whether the fix is
setting MT_LICENSE/MT_LICENSE_PATH to a value the project owner already holds, confirming MassTransit
9.2.0 actually has a no-cost community tier and wiring that in, or deliberately downgrading the pinned
MassTransit/MassTransit.RabbitMQ version in Directory.Packages.props to one predating this licensing
gate, is a product/licensing decision outside what a coding agent should decide unilaterally - flagged
directly to the user rather than worked around silently.

the only unblocked task and the next one to pick up. Before its own acceptance criteria can pass,

[2026-09-05] HOTFIX - MassTransit downgraded to 8.5.10, MT_LICENSE blocker resolved

Not a numbered TASK - a corequisite fix the user explicitly authorized after TASK-046's progress.txt
flagged that MassTransit 9.2.0 hard-crashes all 4 messaging services (Ingestor/Processor/Gateway/
Notification) with `MassTransit.ConfigurationException: License must be specified with
SetLicense/SetLicenseLocation or ... MT_LICENSE/MT_LICENSE_PATH`, which would otherwise make
TASK-047's own acceptance criteria ("docker compose up приводит к рабочему дашборду с живыми
данными") unreachable no matter how correct the compose topology is. Asked the user directly rather
than guessing at a licensing/product decision; they chose: downgrade MassTransit to the last version
before this licensing gate exists, rather than supplying a real license key or a possibly-nonexistent
free-tier env var.

What was done:
  - Directory.Packages.props: MassTransit and MassTransit.RabbitMQ pinned 9.2.0 -> 8.5.10 (the latest
    stable 8.x release; verified via NuGet's flat-container version index that 8.5.10 is the newest
    8.x and that 9.0.0 is the first 9.x). Confirmed MassTransit.RabbitMQ 8.5.10's own nuspec still
    depends on RabbitMQ.Client 7.2.1 - identical to what 9.2.0 required - so the explicit
    RabbitMQ.Client pin and its adjoining comment needed no other change beyond updating the version
    number it references.
  - No .cs changes were needed anywhere - MassTransit 8.x's AddMassTransit/UsingRabbitMq configuration
    surface used by ServiceDefaults/ReadingsTopologyExtensions.cs is source-compatible with 9.x's.

Verification actually performed:
  - dotnet restore + dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln - 0 warnings, 0 errors.
  - dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln - 274/274 passing (same total as before the downgrade - the
    Testcontainers-backed integration tests exercise the real bus against real RabbitMQ and all
    still pass under 8.5.10).
  - Real, disposable infrastructure check (not simulated): rebuilt the Ingestor Docker image against
    the downgraded package, brought up a fresh `docker compose up -d postgres rabbitmq`, and ran
    `docker run --network weakapphandler_backend -e RabbitMq__Host=rabbitmq weakapphandler-ingestor`.
    Log confirms `"Bus started: {HostAddress}","HostAddress":"rabbitmq://rabbitmq/weakapphandler"` -
    no MT_LICENSE exception, the service reaches its normal polling loop (its 404s against
    http://localhost:8080/meters are expected - the weakapp container wasn't started for this
    isolated check). Stack torn down afterward (`docker compose down -v`).

[2026-09-05] TASK-047 - DONE (full docker-compose orchestration, verified against real Docker)

Picked this up as the only unblocked critical-priority task (TASK-046/045 done, TASK-048/049/050 all
chain behind 047). Extended the base docker-compose.yml (weakapp/postgres/rabbitmq only) into the full
stack: added auth/ingestor/processor/gateway/notification/frontend/prometheus/grafana services with
health-check-gated depends_on, correct network placement (edge: auth/gateway/notification/frontend;
backend: all 5 backend services + weakapp/postgres/rabbitmq; observability: all 5 backend services +
prometheus/grafana), named volumes (prometheus-data, grafana-data, alongside the existing
postgres-data/rabbitmq-data), and .env.example additions for every new published port plus Grafana
admin credentials. Every appsettings.json for Auth/Ingestor/Processor/Gateway/Notification already had
explicit "TASK-047 replaces this with the compose service address" comments on every localhost:5091/
5095/5097/8080 reference - these are now overridden via environment: blocks in docker-compose.yml
(ConnectionStrings__*, Auth__JwksUri -> http://auth:8080/..., RabbitMq__Host -> rabbitmq,
Services__Ingestor/Processor__BaseUrl -> in-network names, Cors__FrontendOrigin ->
http://localhost:${FRONTEND_PORT}). Frontend's six VITE_* URLs are baked in at build time
(build.args in the frontend service, referencing ${GATEWAY_PORT}/${NOTIFICATION_PORT}/${AUTH_PORT})
since the browser - not the nginx container - calls them directly; in-network service names would be
unreachable from a host browser, confirmed via a research subagent survey before writing any compose
code, avoiding that trap entirely.

This is NOT a config-only task - real, previously-undiscovered bugs surfaced under actual
docker-compose orchestration. Docker was available and used throughout - every fix below was verified
by actually bringing up the full 10-container stack, not just reasoning about the compose file. Real
bugs found and fixed:

1. No service ever applied EF Core migrations automatically. Every one of Auth/Processor/Notification's
   Program.cs had zero Database.Migrate() call - migrations were "out-of-band, dotnet ef database
   update" per TASK-046's own Dockerfile comments. That directly broke this task's own acceptance
   criterion ("docker compose up -> working dashboard with live data, no additional steps"): a fresh
   Postgres volume left signing_keys/users/etc. simply not existing, and Auth crashed immediately.
   Fixed by adding "await db.Database.MigrateAsync()" at startup in Auth/Processor/Notification's
   Program.cs (Gateway is deliberately excluded - ADR-0003, gateway_ro never migrates). Idempotent, so
   safe against an already-migrated database too. Updated the three Dockerfiles' comments (which
   explicitly claimed the opposite) and Program.cs to match.

2. Discovered immediately after fixing #1: Auth/Processor/Notification share ONE physical Postgres
   database (by design, PRD paragraph 4.2), but EF Core's migrations-history table name defaults to the
   SAME literal "__EFMigrationsHistory" for every DbContext. Whichever service's role migrated first
   created - and exclusively owned - that one table; the other two got "permission denied for table
   __EFMigrationsHistory" the instant their own Program.cs tried to migrate. This is a real,
   pre-existing bug that would have broken "dotnet ef database update" run separately for more than one
   service against the same database too, not something specific to compose - it just never surfaced
   before because no prior session had run more than one service's migrations against the same live
   database in one sitting. Fixed with three distinct MigrationsHistoryTable names
   (__ef_migrations_history_auth/_processor/_notification), added as a "public const string
   MigrationsHistoryTableName" on each of AuthDbContext/CoreDbContext/AlertingDbContext respectively,
   referenced from both the production AddXPersistence/AddXInfrastructure extensions AND every test
   file that manually constructs one of these three DbContexts for its own pre-test migration step (14
   test files across Auth.Tests/Processor.Infrastructure.Tests/Notification.Api.Tests/
   Gateway.Api.Tests/Ingestor.Tests/M2.IntegrationTests - all of them were bypassing the DI-registered
   extension and building DbContextOptionsBuilder directly, so they needed the exact same fix or the
   test suite would have hit the identical "already exists"/"permission denied" collision the first
   time a test brings up more than one of these hosts against a shared fixture database). Verified by
   running the FULL "dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln" afterward: 274/274 passing (same total as before
   this task - no tests lost, none newly skipped).

3. deploy/rabbitmq/definitions.json's per-service ACLs (TASK-043) never actually worked for any
   consumer role. AMQP's queue.bind operation requires WRITE permission on the destination QUEUE and
   READ permission on the source EXCHANGE - but processor/notification/gateway's "write" regex only
   ever matched their shared exchange name, and "read" only ever matched their own queue name(s); the
   two requirements are cross-matched by the protocol, not symmetric, so every one of these three roles
   was structurally unable to bind its own consume queue. This is a real, previously-undiscovered bug in
   TASK-043's own ACLs - confirmed via actual ACCESS_REFUSED errors from a real RabbitMQ 3.13 broker
   (notification: "write access to queue readings.stored refused"; processor: "configure access to
   exchange MassTransit:Fault--WeakAppHandler.Contracts:IngestAttemptRecorded-- refused"). Fixed by
   making write/read mirror the configure regex for each of these three roles (each role's
   write+read+configure now cover exactly its own topology - still fully scoped away from any other
   service's resources, so least-privilege intent is preserved, just correctly symmetric this time).
   Also had to extend processor's pattern specifically to cover
   ReadingsIngestedFault/IngestAttemptRecordedFault (MassTransit's own PascalCase, no-dots naming
   convention for its two Fault<T> consumer queues+exchanges, registered via ConfigureEndpoints since
   they are not bound to an explicit receive endpoint) - the original "_error" suffix guess in
   definitions.json never matched anything real. Also added a fifth "auth" scrape job to
   deploy/prometheus/prometheus.yml, since Auth also calls AddServiceDefaults() and exposes /metrics
   like every other service, but was missing from TASK-045's job list.

4. Confirmed (documented here, NOT fixed - genuinely can't be fixed from the compose file): this
   shared Docker Desktop/Windows machine hit a real, reproducible network-attachment race during
   "docker compose up" roughly 1 time in 2 across about 6 fresh down-then-up cycles this session -
   always the SAME symptom on a DIFFERENT single container each time (confirmed via
   "docker inspect --format {{json .NetworkSettings.Networks}}": the affected container ends up
   attached to only one of its declared networks, with empty IPAddress/EndpointID/Gateway fields on
   that one entry, while "docker compose config" correctly shows all declared networks). Every other
   container in the same "up" always attaches correctly. Recovery is reliable and cheap: remove just
   that one service's container and bring it back up again (NOT a full down with volumes) always fixes
   it, since it forces a clean re-attach. This is a Docker engine/CNI-level race under concurrent
   multi-network attach load, not a compose config defect - "docker compose config" was checked and
   confirmed correct every time this happened. Flagging forward: whoever next runs this compose stack
   on this same shared machine should expect to possibly need that one-container recreate once per
   "up", and should NOT assume the compose file itself is broken if they see it.

Verification actually performed against real, disposable infrastructure (not simulated), using a
throwaway .env with COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME=weakapphandler-verify and host ports offset into the
18000s/19000s/13000 range specifically to avoid colliding with anything else already running on this
shared machine (deleted before finishing, not committed - .env is gitignored anyway):
  - "docker compose up -d" (after a clean volume wipe) brings up all 10 containers healthy with zero
    manual steps, confirmed twice from a clean slate after the fixes above landed.
  - "docker compose restart": all 10 containers came back healthy; a GraphQL "meters" query through
    Gateway returned the exact same 18 auto-registered meters before and after restart, across the
    named postgres-data volume - acceptance criterion 2 directly confirmed, not just inferred from
    volume declarations.
  - Logged in as the seed admin (admin@weakapphandler.local / Admin#12345) via POST /login on the
    published Auth port, got back a real RS256-signed access token; used it as a Bearer token against
    Gateway's /graphql on its own published port - "{ meters { location meterType } }" returned real
    data (18 meters across 6 locations/3 types) that had flowed the entire real pipeline: Ingestor's
    actual poll of the real WeakApp container -> RabbitMQ (readings.exchange) -> Processor (auto
    -registration + persistence under processor_rw) -> Gateway (read via gateway_ro). Gateway's own
    logs confirm it fetched Auth's JWKS from http://auth:8080/.well-known/jwks.json and got a live
    200 - the in-network JWT validation wiring works, not just "the container starts."
  - Exec'd into the frontend container and tried wget/nslookup against postgres/rabbitmq by hostname:
    both fail with "bad address" (DNS resolution itself fails, since frontend is deliberately edge
    -only) - acceptance criterion 3 directly confirmed. (nslookup against "gateway" DOES resolve, which
    is correct and expected: frontend/gateway/notification/auth intentionally share the edge network
    for container-to-container segmentation policy, per the network's own doc comment in
    docker-compose.yml; the browser bypasses Docker networking entirely via published ports for its
    own calls to these services, so this shared edge placement is about segmentation, not about how
    the SPA actually reaches its APIs.)
  - "dotnet build WeakAppHandler.sln" (Debug and Release): 0 warnings, 0 errors, both configurations.
  - "dotnet test WeakAppHandler.sln": 274/274 passing.

NEXT: TASK-048 (retention job) and TASK-049 (CI workflows) both depend only on TASK-047 (now done) and
are the next unblocked tasks - either could be picked up next; TASK-050 additionally depends on 049.
Nothing else needed before starting either. A peer session confirmed via tasks.json that these three
